A file another program, or a second `cma run` with the same --out, puts there meanwhile is read as outside the export guarantee too, like another program writing to the checkpoint file; revision 29 asks the owner (Options considered).
- Still open at c2460a0, against R2 and R53 as revision 29 writes them:
  - The Monzo importer checks a row's Transaction ID before its amount (src/cma/importers/monzo.py:186-193), so an empty or repeated ID on the row that takes money in to the limit is reported without the limit. T04 reads a counted row's amount right after its currency, keeping the ID checks for every row, before its review.
  - The refusal for a field too long to read adds that 'an amount or balance that long would be past the £10,000,000,000,000 limit' (src/cma/ingest.py:141-143), which a field of leading zeros is not, and which R2 now rules out. T04 drops the clause before its review. The MCP server already names such a field only as too long to read (src/cma/mcp_server.py:72).
  - On Windows the lock call gives up after about ten seconds (msvcrt.locking with LK_LOCK, src/cma/cli.py:246), and its error reaches the caller, so a process waiting on another's draft, which can include a model call, fails instead of waiting. T04 makes it wait there as it does on Linux and macOS; CI runs Linux, so the Windows run in Rollout step 3 covers it.
  - An error writing or moving the files after the decision is recorded reaches the caller as itself (src/cma/cli.py:334-338). That is outside R53 since revision 29: the decision is in the checkpoint, and a .part file can stay beside the target.
  - The two new tests in tests/test_cli.py are not marked contracts, which the project's gotcha asks for.
- The lesson is revision 23's, carried to imports and the checkpoint. A refusal that names everything a file holds, and a guarantee that holds under every interleaving, each had one more corner for the next review to find. Stating where the reading stops, and which processes take turns, ends the search.

**What the owner decided on revision 29, and what T04's twenty-eighth to fifty-third reviews and T17's third to fifth reviews showed** (D259 to D286 and D289, verify-154 to verify-185, 2026-11-20 to 2026-11-21)
- D259 answered revision 29's question with its default: an output path another program, or a second `cma run` with the same --out, takes while the review waits is outside the export guarantee, as R53 writes it. D260 approved revision 29.
- T04 was reviewed twenty-six more times, verify-154 to verify-185, and every review failed on R2; the owner took the first twenty-five over (D261 to D284 and D286). In verify-185, at 82758a3, R1, R3, R7, P1, P2 and P6 passed. Each R2 major found one more way the reader met a later part of a file before it named the first problem:
  - *the order of a row's checks* (verify-154, verify-156, verify-161 to verify-163, verify-167): the Transaction ID, a surplus cell or the per-line limit was checked before the currency or the amount, a missing Date raised instead of being named, and surplus cells were ignored;
  - *a byte that is not UTF-8* (verify-156, verify-158 to verify-161, verify-165, verify-169, verify-170, verify-172, verify-173): decoding the whole export first let a later bad byte hide an earlier problem, and each way of reading on past one met one more corner: a file whose lines end in CR alone, a byte-order mark, a multiline header, a bad byte in a record's continuation line or in the first data record;
  - *a field too long to read, or a quoted field left open* (verify-160, verify-164 to verify-171, verify-173): the CSV reader read a whole record before its currency or amount was seen, recovery raised the process-wide field limit, a refusal said such a field would be past the limit, and a quoted field open at the end of the file was accepted;
  - *a row in another currency* (verify-174 to verify-177): skipped by its currency, its unreadable field still stopped the import, or a quoted field left open in it swallowed the rest of the export and the import succeeded, short;
  - *reading past the first problem* (verify-178, verify-180, verify-183, verify-185): recovery parsed the rest of the export, then rebuilt it for each oversized record, then read the body before the first row was checked.
  At 82758a3 the reader reads its records one at a time, in one pass (_records, src/cma/ingest.py:335-425). But the importer reads the whole export as bytes (src/cma/importers/monzo.py:154), decodes it (_decoded, src/cma/ingest.py:230-239) and splits it into lines (:252) before its first record, and holds each record (:352) until the CSV reader has read the next (:359). So a quoted field running over a very large number of lines after a bad first record is read before line 2 is refused (verify-185). The previous release read every record into memory before checking any (src/cma/ingest.py:63-67 and src/cma/importers/monzo.py:123-132 on the integration branch at b777195).
- Two R53 findings came in the same reviews and are answered. Staging names that could overwrite a sibling file (verify-154) are now a unique temporary name (src/cma/cli.py:339 at 82758a3). A lock taken by the checkpoint path as given, so that two paths to one file took two locks (verify-158), is now taken on the resolved path (:238).
- Minors recurred in every review: a scratchpad probe the runner did not run, historical test counts, and the committed example's evidence_note. Since verify-185, test_monzo_reads_nothing_past_the_first_problem (tests/test_money_in.py:1482) counts only the reader's requests for records, not the file's read, decoding or splitting.
- T17, the offline lock and what a prompt may carry (R29, R30, P5, P18), was reviewed three more times, verify-157, verify-182 and verify-184, and every review failed; R30 and P5 passed in verify-184. verify-157 found a run's lock taken from the caller's graph input and a custom rule id reaching the prompt. verify-182 found a bare boolean or a caller's configuration accepted as leave for a hosted request, and LangSmith tracing left on under the lock. Its R29 majors on the MCP server's ASGI apps went round once:
  - verify-122 found that an app built while the lock was off could open its listening socket after the lock was turned on.
  - The owner then refused every ASGI app (478e616), and verify-182 failed that as breaking R53: an embedder that mounted server.sse_app() or server.streamable_http_app() could no longer build it.
  - The owner's c1bbe1a (D285) hands an app out again while the lock is off, behind a check that reads the lock at start-up and before every request and, once the lock is on, fails start-up, answers 503 and closes websockets (LockCheckedASGI, src/cma/mcp_server.py:82-107 at 6b43212); under the lock both factories refuse (:69-79). verify-184 failed R29 again: an embedder's own server with lifespan events turned off binds its socket before anything in the app runs, and a 503 afterwards cannot undo the bind.
  No app can both be handed out and stop a server from binding before the app runs, so R29 and R53 could not both hold there as revision 29 wrote them.
- verify-184's other three majors are answered in 6b43212, the owner's fix for verify-184, not yet reviewed. A reading of firm.yaml decides only for its own run and firm folder: building one hosted client, and the run's hosted requests while the run is going (src/cma/firm.py:128-162). Each run's reading travels in its own config, not in a table by thread (src/cma/graph.py:415-431). A resumed run is untraced if the lock was on when it was drafted or is on now (:419-428). Tests at tests/test_firm.py:867, :892 and :912 mark them. Two corners stay, which revision 30 asks about (Options considered). A lock turned on during a run does not stop that run's later hosted requests, since its reading decides them while it runs. A resume in a long-lived process judges 'on now' by the graph's latest reading where it has one (src/cma/graph.py:427), which can date from before the lock was turned on.
- D289 accepts the two edges as outside the guarantee, to push development forward, and asks for two scope limits, keeping everything else in revision 29. Under R29 and R53: with the lock off, the MCP server hands out its ASGI apps as the previous release did; such an app reads the lock at start-up and before every request and, once the lock is on, fails its start-up, answers every HTTP request with 503 and closes every websocket; under the lock no app is handed out; a listening socket an embedder's own ASGI server opens for a saved app with lifespan events turned off is that server's doing and outside R29, since refusing to hand out an app at all would break R53. Under R2: the loader and `cma import monzo` read the file into memory, decode it and split it into lines before reading its records, then read the records one at a time, in one pass, checking each once the next has been read, and `cma import monzo` stops at its first problem; reading as a stream, and checking a record before the next is read, are outside R2. Revision 30 writes both.
- Five readings, which the requirements' revision note gives in full: an app handed out 'as the previous release did' is the SDK's app behind the lock check, not its class; 'with lifespan events turned off' is one case of a socket the embedder's server opens or holds before start-up; 'before every request' is before every request the app begins to serve; P18 now says its MCP clause through R29; and 'once the next has been read' is at most one record ahead.
- Still open at 82758a3: the docstrings of iter_data and _records (src/cma/ingest.py:281-282 and :338-339) and the comment at src/cma/importers/monzo.py:169-170 say that nothing past the record a caller stops at is read, which the build does not do and R2 no longer asks; T04 rewords them, and the test name, to say that nothing past it is checked, before its review. The Windows lock call still gives up after about ten seconds (src/cma/cli.py:248), which Rollout step 3's Windows run covers.
- Still open at 6b43212: verify-184's minors, a CLI exit status for a failed hosted request that no CLI test proves and a performance claim the runner does not measure.
- The lesson is revision 23's, at the edge of the package. A guarantee about what another program does with what the package hands it, or about how much of a file the reader touches before it names the first problem, cannot be kept by the package's checks, and each review found the next way round them. Stating where the package's reach ends, at the embedder's own server and at the file read whole, ends the search.

**What the owner decided on revision 30, and what T04's fifty-fourth to fifty-seventh reviews and T17's sixth to ninth reviews showed** (D290 to D298 and D301, verify-186 to verify-193, 2026-11-21)
- D290 answered revision 30's first question with its default: the app the MCP server hands out is the SDK's app behind a lock check, a wrapper, as 6b43212 builds, and R53 already says that code that checks its class sees another class. D291 answered the second with its default: a lock turned on takes effect from the next run, a run keeps the lock its reading found as it started, a resume reads firm.yaml as it resumes, and R29 and P18 are to say so in the next revision. D292 approved revision 30.
- T04 was reviewed four more times against revision 30, and every review failed on R2. The owner took the first three over (D294, D295, D297), and D301 followed the fourth.
  - verify-187, at 82758a3, found two things. The loader read the whole sequence of records before it checked any record's values. And a quoted field left open in a Monzo export's header was reported as an export with no transactions, naming neither the field nor its line. bb5198c checks each record as it is read and refuses an open-quote header.
  - verify-188, at bb5198c, found that a record with a byte that is not UTF-8 skipped the open-quote check. A row in another currency whose open quote took in later GBP rows was therefore skipped, and the export imported short. It also found that the loader checked the running balance only once every record was read. 2b0667f checks date order and the balance as each record is read, and stops reading at an open record with a bad byte.
  - verify-190, at 2b0667f, found that a newest-first comparison for a record was made only once the record after it was handed out, by which time a second later record had been read. It also found that repeated blank headers let a clean cell overwrite a bad byte under the first. bedf5ff hands records out as it reads them and finds bad bytes by the record's own cells.
  - verify-192, at bedf5ff, found two more. The balance check compares a record with the last record before it that has a balance (follow, src/cma/ingest.py:489-511), so across a record with no usable balance it is made two or more records on. And the Monzo importer walks the balance only once every row is checked and sorted (src/cma/importers/monzo.py:291-326), so with a bad date on line 4 it names the date and never checks line 3's walked balance. verify-192 passed R1, R3, R7, P1, P2 and P6.
- Minors recurred: a probe script the runner did not run, historical test counts, and a note that the committed example lacks evidence_note, which it has. verify-187 added that test_monzo_reads_nothing_past_the_first_problem bounds the reader's requests, which is less than the importer's comment claims. verify-190 added that an oversized --opening-balance escapes as Python's own integer-formatting error on 3.11 and later (src/cma/importers/monzo.py:304). verify-192 added that M10 can round an exact half differently from the previous release. A £20,001.00 card settlement against a £20,000.00 loan repayment gives 1.00005, which the previous release's float round makes 1.0001 and the exact half-even rounding makes 1.0000 (src/cma/metrics.py:438). R10 and P7 keep M1 to M16's values only on committed cases and fixtures, none of which holds such a ratio, and R54 keeps their evidence, which does not move.
- From verify-188 on, every R2 major was a check that relates records to one another, made later than one record ahead. Revision 30 said that each record is checked once the next has been read, and each review read that for every check that touches a record. Such a check cannot always be made then. Which way a file runs is known from its dates, and the loader decides it from its first and last (src/cma/ingest.py:576-579), so a newest-first file's order and balance problems can be told from an oldest-first file's only once the file is read. A balance across a record with none can be checked only at the next record that has one. And the Monzo importer walks the balance in date and time order, which it knows only once every row is read.
- D301 clarifies the limit and keeps everything else in revision 30. The one-record-ahead limit is about each record's own values (its cells, its amount and balance against the line limit, a bad byte, a field too long to read, a quoted field left open), which are checked once the next record has been read at most. Checks that relate records to one another are made from the records read, once reading has stopped: which way the file runs and whether its dates keep to it, whether each balance follows from the one before (across a record whose balance is missing or unreadable, as before this change), the money in and money out totals, and the Monzo importer's walk of the balance after sorting by date and time. They are not limited to one record ahead, and they keep no order with a record's own problem beyond R2's naming of the first problem the Monzo importer meets in a record. Revision 31 writes this into R2.
- Revision 31 makes four readings, which the requirements' revision note gives in full. A record's own values are every check that needs that record alone, its id and date among them, and an id that repeats an earlier one's is checked with them. 'Once reading has stopped' is once the reader has read the last record it reads. R2's naming of the Monzo importer's first problem is revision 29's, with money in or money out at the limit. And revision 30's 'each record' now reads 'each record's own values'.
- bedf5ff already makes the checks as R2 now says. Three things are still open there. The docstrings of iter_data and _records (src/cma/ingest.py:286 and :343) and the comment at src/cma/importers/monzo.py:169 still say that nothing past the first problem is read, which revision 30 asked T04 to reword. The oversized --opening-balance still escapes as Python's own error. And the Windows lock call still gives up after about ten seconds, which Rollout step 3's Windows run covers.
- T17 was reviewed four more times, verify-186 at 6b43212, verify-189 at b5779ab, verify-191 at 2e85d1c and verify-193 at 25db103, and every review failed. The owner took the first three over (D293, D296, D298); 9b10daa, the fix for the fourth, is not yet reviewed. R30 passed in verify-193. The majors fell into three runs and one more.
  - *A reading of firm.yaml across a lock change* (verify-186, verify-189, verify-191). An unused reading taken while the lock was off could allow a hosted client after the firm locked. A reading could be reused across graphs, and a resume on the same compiled graph could trace after the lock was turned on. A run's first reading was never checked for freshness, and a firm.yaml replaced with the same length and modification time was taken for the old one. A second graph invocation inside a suspended stream inherited the first's tracing. b5779ab, 2e85d1c and 25db103 bind each reading to firm.yaml's bytes as it stood, require a run's reading to be current, and mark no run for a stream. These are the corners D291 decides, which R29 and P18 do not yet state.
  - *The MCP server's apps and runners* (verify-186, verify-191, verify-193). A saved app read the lock before the lifespan start-up arrived. The native HTTP runners gave Uvicorn the SDK's raw app. And a native runner that read the lock before the lock was turned on reaches its bind under the lock (src/cma/mcp_server.py:111 at 25db103). Since 25db103 the native runners serve the lock-checked app, so a request on such a socket gets 503 and no tool runs. The window between the runner's check and its bind remains.
  - *A custom policy's rule ids in the prompt* (all four reviews, under P5). T17 keeps a custom rule's id in the prompt's rule table only when the rule is the packaged one, and the comparison failed four times: by id spelling, then without the referral bands, then without on_fail and when_missing. In verify-193 it failed without the policy's provenance: an exact copy of the packaged R2 under another policy name, with R2 used as a sentinel in the case's descriptions and profile, put R2 in the prompt (src/cma/graph.py:196 at 25db103). A sentinel equal to a packaged rule id appears in every prompt drawn up with the packaged policy, whatever the build does, so P5 as written cannot hold for it.
  - verify-193 also handed a run a LangChainTracer in its callbacks, which stayed active under the lock and could export the run's data (src/cma/graph.py:535 at 25db103). 9b10daa takes a LangSmith tracer out of a run's callbacks under the lock.
  Minors recurred. A claim of one firm.yaml read per run, where the build hashes and parses the file. A CLI exit 4 on a failed hosted request that no CLI test asserts. A dispatch_used test the runner did not run. Redirects followed inside one SDK call. And two concurrent CLI starts passing the unused-id check on the T17 branch, which lacks T04's lock (R53 since revision 29).
- D301 keeps everything else in revision 30, so R29, R30, P5 and P18 do not change. Revision 31 asks the owner about the three runs, the callback and when R29 and P18 should say D291 (Options considered). T12 is still blocked at 04ff28a, with D217 pending.
- The lesson is revision 23's, at the reader and the config file. A scope that bounds 'each record' is read for every check that touches a record, and a check of firm.yaml leaves a window before the step it guards, as a lock-file check does. A requirement has to say which checks a bound covers and where a window is allowed, or each review finds the next one.

**What the repo already does** (re-read at 43f3e57; the lines revision 1 cited are where it said). It imports a five-column CSV or a Monzo export with strict validation (src/cma/ingest.py, src/cma/importers/monzo.py). An ordered regex categoriser never guesses (src/cma/categorise.py). No rule mentions cash, so a cash deposit today falls to OTHER and shows in M16 (src/cma/categorise.py:37-50, 64-66). It computes 16 figures with evidence ids (src/cma/metrics.py:248-292); revenue is the set REVENUE_CATEGORIES (src/cma/models.py:33), which M2, M3, M4 and M9 read (src/cma/metrics.py:121, 172). It applies an illustrative YAML policy to pass, refer or fail (src/cma/policy.py:83-123, src/cma/policies/uk_sme_default.yaml), and the guard already accepts a threshold tagged [Rn] when it is introduced as a limit (src/cma/guard.py:189-201). A LangGraph pipeline drafts the memo, checks it with the guard and stops for a named reviewer (src/cma/graph.py). There is an offline template model (src/cma/llm.py:26-69) and an MCP server (src/cma/mcp_server.py).

On the T03 branch (cited at ecec4ca for revision 4; not merged) the v2 table is data with a stage and a protected flag on every rule, cash paid in is no longer protected, and the payment rail is a cue with no category (src/cma/rules/v2.yaml:117-122 and 214-236 on that branch). A credit's verdict is computed in two stages, and an ambiguous credit records `v2:conflict:` and its candidates (src/cma/categorise.py:389-404 and 446-462). Revision 4 found three gaps there: a cue with a bucket returned before the rail-plus-cash check (:399), the candidates came from the deciding stage only (:399, :403), and word boundaries counted only ASCII letters and digits (:121-126). The sixth attempt closed them and left the two that D64 settles. The T03 worktree (branch at b4b511c for revision 6, at 5bac88c for revisions 7 and 8, at 79965d4 for revision 9, at d507393 for revision 10, reviewed as verify-53, and at b6494fd for revisions 11 and 12, after D100) also holds D70-D72: the cheque is a line-type cue with no category, no rule carries an exception, advance wording sits on the loan list, and listed names and profile names share one separator set (src/cma/categorise.py:206-217 at b6494fd). Since the owner's D78 take-over, a profile name the wording matches joins an ambiguous credit's candidates instead of settling it. Since D86 every kind it matches joins, and a credit both a director's and an own account's names match is ambiguous whatever the table finds; since the D90 change (e24f466) such a credit records every rule it matched (src/cma/categorise.py:730-760 at b6494fd, :750). README.md:78 there still says the profile settles the credit. A protected verdict outranks a profile name there, for credits (:739-741) and for debits (:699-704), as C1 has said since revision 3 and R5 and R6 say since revision 8. Since D94-D97, the validator compiles every form of a pattern the matcher uses. It also refuses back-references and conditional groups, and allows the bank-code flag only on the rule whose whole pattern is a bank return code listed in code, with no names (:94-101, :253-321; src/cma/rules/v2.yaml:63-69). A rule's pattern and names are scanned apart, and at each start the longest match any of them, or any alternative inside a pattern, can make is kept (:557-611, D102). The bank-code rule is never counted as inside another rule's longer match (:625-627, D101). Both were verify-53's majors at d507393. The round-trip pass undoes only customer transfers, and M7, M15 and the M16 numerator read returned_dd, revenue_transfer and refund_in (src/cma/metrics.py:30, 156, 207), so the category a credit gets inside its bucket changes figures. Reversal linking compares payees after setting aside a fixed list of bank prefixes that has UNPAID DD but not RETURNED DD (src/cma/categorise.py:767-807). At 78556a7 (the owner's review-21 fix, not yet reviewed) the code has moved again: the table checks are validate_ruleset (:356-501), which refuses an exception on every v2 rule (:423-432) and a lookahead or lookbehind in a v2 pattern (:433-439, with _has_lookaround at :259-276); rule_matches still reads an exception (:659-663), which only the frozen v1 table can now carry; the longest match at each start is _spans (:754-773); credit_matches with its longer-phrase filter is :776-796, its two-way exemption for cash paid-in wording and a payment-method cue :786-792 and :803-806; credit_verdict is :844-861, _classify_credit :911-941, reversal linking :948-989 and the round-trip pass :995-1019. At 2c72469 (the owner's review-22 fix, 5c9a5bd, and D137's check, neither yet reviewed) the lines move by 22 from :279, by 29 from :462 and by 43 after credit_matches: validate_ruleset is :378-532, refusing an anchor on every v2 pattern but the bank's code (:462-468, with _has_anchor at :279-298); _spans is :783-803; credit_matches is :805-839, which settles which rules count by repeating its filter (:815-839); credit_verdict is :887-906, _classify_credit :954-986, reversal linking :1007-1032 and the round-trip pass :1038-1062. At 4b3b21c (the owner's review-23 fix, not yet reviewed) the lines up to credit_matches are as at 2c72469. credit_matches is :805-844 and settles occurrence by occurrence (:815-843), and every line after it moves by 5: credit_verdict is :892-911, _classify_credit :959-991, reversal linking :1012-1037 and the round-trip pass :1043-1067. At 9042fb4 (the owner's review-24 fix, not yet reviewed) validate_ruleset is :378-534 and applies every check a v2 table gets to every table but one that bears v1's id and calls itself frozen (:381-384), whose bytes load_ruleset compares with the hash pinned in code (:665-673); credit_matches is :809-849, credit_verdict :896-915, _classify_credit :963-995, reversal linking :1016-1046 with its bank prefixes at :1000-1002, and the round-trip pass :1047-1073. At 7cb2e71 (the owner's review-25 fix, not yet reviewed) _is_bank_code_text (:368-371) is the type test behind the two bank-code membership checks (:440, :479); validate_ruleset is :384-540, and still gathers the codes the bank-code rules carry by hashing each such rule's pattern (:534); load_ruleset is :667-694 and compares a pinned table's bytes before it parses the table (:676-691); credit_matches is :829-869, credit_verdict :916-935, _classify_credit :983-1015, reversal linking :1036-1066 with its bank prefixes at :1020-1022, and the round-trip pass :1067-1093. At 5139e22 (the owner's review-26 fix, not yet reviewed) validate_ruleset is :384-541, and gathers the codes the bank-code rules carry only from patterns that are text (:534-535). load_ruleset is :667-697. It refuses any failure in parsing or checking a changed v1 as a changed frozen table first (:685-688), and any other failure that is not the table's own refusal as a malformed value that could not be checked, naming no rule (:689-691). credit_matches starts at :832, credit_verdict at :919 (with the bank's code at step 2 at :925) and _classify_credit at :986, and the reversal-linking prefixes are at :1024. At 5f5bb2e (the owner's review-27 fix, not yet reviewed) validate_ruleset is :384-561. It takes the hash of the table's bytes and spares the exception checks only for v1 as its bytes were verified (:390-393, :449-474), keeps the checks only the maintained lists need for every table but one loaded as v1 that calls itself frozen (:475-505, :508-517, :538-543), and builds every well-typed rule's matchers while it checks the table (:544-557, with _well_typed_text and _build_matchers at :563-583), which ruleset_from_data reuses (:638-656). load_ruleset is :707-737. credit_matches starts at :872, credit_verdict at :959 (with the bank's code at step 2 at :965) and _classify_credit at :1026, and the reversal-linking prefixes are at :1064. At 4c017d4 (the owner's review-38 fix, not yet reviewed) validate_ruleset is :397-622. It spares the exception checks only where a digest that matches the pin is given (:403-407). It names a key given more than once and checks every rules list given (:415-423), and checks each value of a repeated field for exceptions, lookarounds and anchors (:474-500, with _variants at :794-803). It builds each rule's matchers part by part (:573-618). The loader keeps a repeated key as an unbuilt value holding every value given (_UniqueKeyLoader, :735-770; _Repeated, :786-791). ruleset_from_data is :700-728 and load_ruleset :868-898. credit_matches starts at :1056, credit_verdict at :1143 (with the bank's code at step 2 at :1149) and _classify_credit at :1210, and the reversal-linking prefixes are at :1248. At 3ae10cc (the owner's fix for verify-117, reviewed as verify-119) validate_ruleset is :419-691; the loader keeps a key given more than once as an unbuilt value holding every value given (_UniqueKeyLoader, :821-856, with _Repeated at :872-877); and load_ruleset (:960-989) turns any failure that is not the table's own refusal into one (:977-983). credit_matches starts at :1147, credit_verdict at :1234 and _classify_credit at :1301.

On the T12 branch (eb19a56 for revision 5, c40ff2c for revision 6, e6b34e8 for revision 7, a65685d for revision 8, fe880a9 for revision 9, b5a85e4 for revision 10, 6e73302 for revision 12, b428d56 for revision 13, 1d16873 for revision 14, reviewed as verify-68, cd298cc for revision 15, reviewed as verify-70, and f5436bb now, the owner's review-23 fix, not yet reviewed; not merged; the lines below are at cd298cc, and the paragraph after them says where f5436bb moves them) the case log is src/cma/case.py:
- one canonical JSON line per entry, each carrying the hash of the one before;
- a head record beside the log (log.jsonl.head) holding the entry count and every entry's hash, compared on each load (_check, :271) and created empty before the first entry's line, only where no head record exists: the staged empty record is linked into place, which fails if one is there, so it is never renamed over one (_create_empty_head, :888-906, called at :939-943; revision 15, after verify-68);
- a torn entry past the recorded entries that blocks appends until someone confirms dropping it, through a rewrite of the log to a temporary file and a rename (drop_torn_entry and _replace_torn, :813-866), while a recorded entry cut short, or any entry with no head record beside it, is damage that is named and never dropped (_check). A complete line past the recorded entries whose link does not name the last recorded entry breaks the chain, and is broken, never a torn write to drop (_check);
- two lock files beside the log, neither holding data. The gate (log.jsonl.gate) is what a writer passes alone and briefly to take the case, leaving it at once whether it took the case or was refused (_enter, :666-697, which notes the gate's identity as the writer passes it). The case lock (log.jsonl.lock) is what the writer holding the case holds alone (_take_case_lock, :699). Both are recognised by the file itself, not the path's spelling. A check holds them shared, on Windows through LockFileEx (:1030-1070). A writer that finds the gate held by checks waits for them, and one that finds another writer holding the case waits a moment and is refused (writer(), :622). writer() releases by depth, only the level its own entry added, so an interrupt between taking the lock and noting it leaves no lock (D120), and a holder is a per-thread number that is never reused (_holder_id and _thread_token, :389-406, D118). A writer refuses a lock file replaced between opening and locking it (_take_case_lock). It re-checks that both lock files are still the ones it passed and locked, the gate as well as the case lock (_require_lock_in_place, :732-745; D136), immediately before every write call of the line (:953), before it links the empty head record into place (:899) and before every attempt of each rename that publishes a head record or a repaired log (_replace, :995; D108, D114, D116). Each write stages in a temporary file of its own (_staging_file, :987, D118). One save runs at a time per holder (_one_save, :769-783), and an append refuses if the log's length or bytes changed since its check (:947-950), as R42 says since revision 13. Since 1d16873 (D125) a save publishes its head record only if the head record still lists what its check read (_write_head, :908-931), and is told recorded only after reading back an intact log that holds its entry where the head record lists it (_publish, :962-978). A repair compares the log's bytes with its check before it reads the log (:836-838), and re-checks, before its log rename, that both lock files are in place, that the log's bytes are still the ones its check read and that the head record is unchanged (:843-851), as R42 says since revision 14 (verify-68 found the length alone compared at 1d16873).
Its reader, CaseLog.verify (:444), holds the gate shared for each attempt and then tries the case lock shared. If the case lock is free, or there is no case lock file behind the gate, it reads once and judges the whole log. If the case lock is held, it judges a steady read and tries the case lock again: it judges the whole log if the writer let go, and otherwise reports the recorded entries and the rest as being saved. A check that finds the gate busy, because a writer is passing it or paused there, tries the case lock directly and reads holding it if it is free (D98). If the case is held too, a steady read names a fault at or before the recorded entries, but only within a head record it read (D105, D116), and otherwise the check tries again. With no gate file, a steady read stands unless a gate appears meanwhile. When the retries run out, it reports being saved and claims nothing only if some attempt saw the case lock held, and otherwise that the log could not be checked (D120). A lock that cannot be taken or tested blames no other window (_untested, :572). `cma case check` (src/cma/cli.py) loads it and exits 0, 3 or 4 as D75 decided, 4 for both answers that check nothing. At f5436bb (the owner's review-23 fix, not yet reviewed) the lines from :545 move by 23 and those from :987 by 33. After each read the reader compares each lock file it holds with what its path names now, and takes the read again if one was removed or replaced (:476-480, used at :489, :498, :505 and :509); the read a busy-gate check takes holding the case lock alone is not yet compared (:527-533). A save whose read-back fails takes back the head record it published, when it is still its own, by publishing the head record its check read through the same re-checked rename (_publish, :985-1011, the take-back at :999-1011). At 3076547 (the owner's review-24 fix, 0d339cc, and D143's answer, neither yet reviewed), a holder's read counts only while the case lock it holds and the gate it passed are still the case's, and otherwise goes on as any other reader's (verify, :451-482). The read a busy-gate check takes holding the case lock alone compares that lock file with its path (:553-558). _publish (:1017-1049) takes back its head record checking only that the head record is still its own, whatever the lock files (:1032-1042), and raises SaveNotConfirmed (:107-110) when the take-back is not made (:1043-1047). At 7c8fba2 (the owner's review-25 fix, not yet reviewed) the busy-gate read takes its case lock missing at the comparison after its read as removed and looks again (:553-562). _publish (:1019-1063) maps an error from the file system in publishing the head record to not recorded or not confirmed (:1031-1041), and re-checks both lock files and the head record before the take-back's rename, answering not confirmed when the take-back is not made (:1049-1061). At 51c8b68 (the owner's review-26 fix, 9c265a3, and D154's mapping, neither yet reviewed) a refusal raised while the line is being written answers not recorded, saying part of its entry was written, whenever the log is longer than the save's check found it (:1024-1035); every operating-system error raised from an append's or a repair's steps after its check reaches the caller as the save not recorded, naming the error (_os_errors_as_not_recorded, :842-852, around _write at :880 and _replace_torn at :897); and _publish's own answer, not recorded or not confirmed, stands where the head record is published (:1056-1066). The reads a save makes only to check, the append's byte comparison (:1018), a repair's re-check (:921), the head record's re-check (:995), the read-back (:1067), the take-back (:1075) and the lock files' examination (:795-799), still turn the error into a value. At 1e35352 (the owner's review-27 fix, de6a56e, and D159's reads, neither yet reviewed) a refusal at the head rename after the line is reported in the save's own words: its entry was written and the save was not recorded (_publish, :1059-1064). The reads a save makes only to check raise the operating system's error instead of turning it into a value: the log's digest (_log_digest, :957-963, at :927 and :1024), the head record's re-checks (_read_head with raise_os, :247-259, at :927 and :1001), the read-back (:1076-1085), and the examination of both lock files, where only a missing file reads as removed or replaced (_require_lock_in_place, :793-808). _os_errors_as_not_recorded (:848-858) and _publish (:1050-1105) name the error. The take-back still quotes its refusal in its not-confirmed report (:1100-1104). At 7fb3a23 (the owner's review-28 fix, 26bc51e, and D164's wording, neither yet reviewed) the lock check's refusal ends 'the step was refused' (_require_lock_in_place, :794-811). _write_head notes when its rename is done (:1009), and _publish (:1055-1122) answers not recorded unless that rename was made (:1071-1081). It names a take-back that is not made in the save's own words, neither quoting nor chaining its refusal (:1106-1121). At 523c5df (the owner's review-39 fix, not yet reviewed) a hold's check of its lock files (_Hold.in_place, :438-453) looks at both paths, then at the link counts of both files held, then at both paths again. It serves both the holder's read (verify, :512-523) and a writer's re-check before each step (_require_lock_in_place, :839-859). Any other reader looks at each file it holds and then the first again (:540-549). One save at a time is a single step (_one_save, :887-902). _os_errors_as_not_recorded (:904-950) reports whatever stops a save once its line has started to reach the log as the save's own answer. Every signal whose handler is Python code is held off from publishing the head record to the take-back (_interrupts_deferred, :1252-1291). At 43332c6 (the owner's fix for verify-116, reviewed as verify-118) the holder's and the ordinary reader's lock checks are at :461 and :503, the nested-save refusal at :926, the expected-position conflict at :995, a repair's checks at :1043, and the read-back and the take-back at :1244 and :1294; whatever stops a save once its line has started to reach the log is the save's own answer (:955-993). The integration branch (c2a2f96) has no case log and no profile names yet.

**Gap against the reply**

| The underwriter asked for | Today | Gap |
|---|---|---|
| Genuine trading income separated from transfers, director money and cash | Revenue is claimed narrowly; every other credit, cash included, is one OTHER pile in M16 | No breakdown of money in; cash takings, director loans, own-account transfers and refunds look alike |
| Real acquirers and merchant cash advances recognised | Only synthetic acquirer names (README:125-126) | Real card revenue lands in M16 and refers the case |
| Large cash deposits highlighted | Nothing recognises cash | Cash is neither counted as takings nor flagged |
| Unusual credits put in front of me | When two rules match a credit, the first in rule order wins | A guess looks like a fact, and can land in trading receipts |
| Click any number to its lines | Evidence ids in a JSON file; an MCP tool | Nowhere a person can click |
| Correct categories | Not possible | No override and no record of who changed what |
| Monthly credits, debits and average balance | Period totals; lowest balance only | No monthly table, no average balance |
| A summary for my memo | The agent's own memo, with a pass/refer/fail verdict | Not in the firm's field order; nothing next to the verdict says the thresholds are illustrative |
| Runs on our machine, with an audit trail | The offline model exists; outputs are git-ignored | Nothing stops a hosted model; no tamper-evident log |
| Open Banking feed, PDFs | CSV and Monzo only (README:111) | No adapter |

## Options considered

**Option A: local review workspace (chosen).** The cma package serves a web page on the underwriter's machine, reachable only from that machine (loopback). Every figure, bucket and monthly cell opens its lines, and the lines needing judgement come first. The underwriter re-categorises lines with a reason and the figures layer recomputes, along with the indicators and the illustrative policy check. A named sign-off writes a memo-ready pack and a self-contained HTML record. The model is off by default.

**Option B: static HTML report plus a CLI overrides file.** Each run writes one self-contained HTML file with click-through. Corrections are typed into an overrides file and the run repeated; sign-off stays at the terminal. This is the cheapest option and needs no server. *It lost because* the two things the underwriter asked for beyond click-through, correcting categories and a usable summary, would happen in a file-editing loop outside the page, and the underwriter would see the effect of a correction on the figures and the policy check only after a re-run. It is kept as the format of the signed record (the pack's HTML).

**Option C: spreadsheet as the interface.** Export lines and figures to a workbook, let the underwriter edit a category column, and re-import. It is familiar: they add things up in a spreadsheet today. *It lost because* a figure loses its link to its evidence as soon as a cell is edited or copied. The tool also cannot tell a category correction from an edited amount, and re-importing a hand-edited workbook is the error-prone step the bet is meant to remove. A monthly-summary CSV stays as an export.

**Option D: hosted service (SaaS) now.** One hosted web app per firm; D14 frames the business as SaaS. *It lost for this bet because* applicant data would leave the firm, which is the blocker the reply names. A hosted pilot also needs sign-in, tenancy and a data processing agreement before the approver will look at it. Option A's workspace is the same web page, so hosting it later is a change of deployment, not a rewrite. The owner confirmed local-first (D29).

**Option E: let a model categorise the unclear lines.** Send unexplained lines to a model and accept the buckets it suggests. *It lost because* it puts payer-written text and names in a prompt, which raises GDPR and prompt-injection risk from whoever wrote the payment reference. It also breaks ADR 001 ('the model receives a table, not a statement'), and a category chosen by a model is exactly the kind of invented judgement the underwriter fears. Deterministic rules, rules the firm learns and per-line overrides do this job instead.

**Sub-decision: how to show what is unexplained.** (i) Redefine M16 to mean unexplained only. (ii) Keep M16's value (money in that is not trading receipts), rename it to say so, and add a figure for each bucket plus the unexplained share (M26). *Chose (ii)*: a memo already signed that cites M16 must keep its meaning, and ids are stable.

**Sub-decision: how cash deposits count (D31).** (i) Out of trading receipts until the underwriter moves them (revision 1). (ii) In trading receipts, with the cash inside them reported as its own figure (M18) and indicators on large deposits, cash-heavy months and cash withdrawn then paid back in. (iii) A firm setting that chooses (i) or (ii). *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer. (i) lost because it understates a cash business such as a café and makes the underwriter add back every ordinary deposit by hand. (iii) lost because M2 would then mean different things in different packs, so a signed memo citing M2 could not be read without knowing the setting; a firm that wants a deposit out moves it with an override and a reason. What (ii) gives up: personal cash paid in by an owner lifts M2, M9 and M10 unless an indicator catches it or a person moves it (see Consequences).

**Sub-decision: how the policy outcome is shown (D28).** (i) Hidden unless the firm loads its own policy (revision 1). (ii) Shown from the packaged policy in the workspace and the pack, with the illustrative label inside the same panel and the same pack section, behind a firm setting that is on by default. (iii) Removed from the review flow; `cma run` keeps it. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer. Putting the label in the same section, not in a footnote or only in the HTML record, means copying the section copies the label. (i) lost because the firm has no policy file to load (E8 is open), so the outcome would stay hidden for the whole showcase. (iii) lost because the showcase would no longer show the whole chain, from a traced figure to a rule result that a person must act on.

**Sub-decision: how sign-off works.** (i) Drive the existing LangGraph review interrupt from the workspace. (ii) Make the case folder the source of truth, and make sign-off a case-log event bound to a snapshot hash. *Chose (ii)*. The figures are a pure function of the case's inputs, so a checkpoint would hold nothing extra, and re-running the graph after every override would also re-run the draft node. ReviewDecision's name and notes validation is reused, not copied. `cma run` keeps its interrupt unchanged. Revision 2 settled the order: the underwriter signs a preview, and the pack is written once, at sign-off, from that preview.

**Sub-decision: where the categorisation rules live.** (i) Keep them as code and add more. (ii) Move the rule tables into packaged, validated data files like the policy YAML; the matching passes (reversals, round trips, pairing, profile names) stay in code. *Chose (ii)*: the lists of acquirers, lenders, cash wording and gambling operators are what changes most often, the v1 table can be kept as data for rollback, and a firm's own rules use the same format.

**Sub-decision: how the categoriser settles wording that points two ways (D52, revision 3).** (i) First match in table order, with exceptions inside rules to steer known collisions: v1, and T03's attempts before revision 3. (ii) Two stages, with disagreement as a verdict of its own: line-type cues decide when any cue with a bucket matches; names decide only when none does; within the deciding stage, matches in two or more buckets make the credit ambiguous. A match inside a longer match from another rule counts only as the longer one, and the bank's own returned-payment code settles the line type alone. (iii) Any two buckets anywhere, cue or name, make the credit ambiguous. *Chose (ii)*. (i) lost because order is a silent choice. Each review round found a new pair of lists it got wrong, and an exception that hides one list's cue when another's is present (HMRC hidden by savings wording) turns a disagreement into a guess nobody sees. (iii) lost because a cue says what the line is and a name says who sent it. Every refund from PayPal, Amazon or an acquirer, and every bounced direct debit to HMRC, would go to the queue though its wording is not in doubt, and it would undo the owner's D46 rule that the cue wins. What (ii) gives up: a cue in payer-written text outranks names, so a payer who writes REFUND moves their credit to refunds. Every cue is non-trading, so this can only understate trading receipts, never invent them. The owner confirmed this reading (D53).

**Sub-decision: the bank's code inside a longer listed phrase (revision 10, after verify-53; D101, in R4 since revision 11).** R4 says a returned-payment code the bank prints at the start of the line settles the line type on its own, and that a match inside a longer listed phrase counts only as the longer phrase. A table edit can make them meet: UNPAID DD TO SAVINGS on the own-pot list contains the code. (i) The bank's code settles the line type before the longer-phrase rule applies: a listed bank code at the start of the line is never counted as inside another rule's phrase, so UNPAID DD TO SAVINGS is a returned payment whatever list holds the longer phrase. (ii) Refuse at load a table in which another rule's names begin with a listed bank code: the clauses then never meet through names, but a pattern on another rule could still match there, and no validator can decide that for every pattern. (iii) The longer phrase wins, and R4's bank-code clause gives way. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D101), which verify-53 expects and R4 says since revision 11 (D106). The code is printed by the bank in its own place, not written by the payer, and R4 gives it the line type on its own; a table edit should not be able to turn it into payer wording. What (i) costs: R4's longer-phrase clause has one exception, which R4 states since revision 11, and a table author who lists a phrase beginning with the code gets the code's verdict, not theirs. Both readings are non-trading, so M2 does not move.

**Sub-decision: alternatives inside one pattern (revision 10, after verify-53; D102, in R4 since revision 11).** Since D97 a rule's pattern and its names are scanned apart and the longest match at each start is kept. Inside one pattern, alternatives that start at the same place still meet: Python's engine returns the first that matches, not the longest. (i) The first alternative that matches, as d507393 does. (ii) The longest match any of the pattern's alternatives can make at that start, leftmost-longest as POSIX defines it, so the order of alternatives inside a pattern picks nothing, as rule order picks nothing between rules. (iii) v2 patterns may not use alternatives or optional groups; a rule that needs several phrases lists them as names, which are already matched longest first. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D102), which R4 says since revision 11 (D106). (i) lost on verify-53: R4 says a match inside a longer listed phrase counts only as the longer phrase, and an author who writes both phrases in one pattern has listed both. (iii) lost because five shipped patterns use alternatives (LOAN or FINANCE twice, the drawings and dividend words, bank charges, payroll) and the invoice reference uses an optional group; moving them to names changes their whole-word and separator behaviour, and a ban on syntax cannot be complete. What (ii) costs: the standard library's engine finds the first match, not the longest. So the matcher tries, at each start of a rule part with alternatives or a lazy repeat, the latest end first (D100, built with the standard library). That is up to one extra attempt per character after the start, on short descriptions, and the 2-second target at 10,000 lines (R52) still applies. It also makes a match as long as its pattern allows, which matters for a pattern that can match across words (next sub-decision). No shipped pattern has two alternatives that start at the same place, so no shipped verdict moves.

**Sub-decision: patterns that can match across words (revision 11; D110).** Since D102 a rule's match is the longest its pattern can make at a start, and R4 counts a match inside a longer one only as the longer one. Consider a v2 pattern with a repeat that can match across a space, such as .* or [A-Z ]+. Its match would reach as far along the line as the pattern's end allows, and every other list's wording inside that reach would count only as its match. A card-list pattern written SQUARE.* would make SQUARE UK CASH ADVANCE a card settlement in trading receipts, not ambiguous. A greedy repeat did this before D102; D102 makes a lazy one do it too. (i) Refuse such a pattern at load, naming the rule, as D94 and D96 refuse forms whose meaning would surprise. The test is a repeat whose body can match both a space and a letter or digit. A repeat of spaces alone, digits alone or letters alone is allowed, so the invoice reference loads. (ii) Allow it, and state that a table author who writes one takes in what follows. (iii) End such a match where another rule's match begins. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D110). (ii) lost because a table edit would then hide one list's wording behind another's, the silent choice D52 ended, and verify-53's two majors were table edits. (iii) lost because one rule's length would then depend on every other rule, and an edit to any list could change any match. What (i) costs: a phrase with a gap is listed as names or written with whitespace as its gap. No shipped pattern has such a repeat, so no shipped verdict moves; T03 built the check in ef057ad.

**Sub-decision: exceptions inside a v2 rule (revision 13, after verify-65; D126, in R4 since revision 14).** A rule may carry an `unless`, which stops it matching when that pattern also matches, or a `requires`. D49 allowed an exception only when it excludes wording that is on no list, and since D70 no shipped v2 rule has one; at 1b592b6 the validator refuses them on the bank-code rule only (D115). verify-65 added `unless: FPS` to the cash rule: FPS CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE lost its cash wording, the gateway decided, and the credit counted as a card settlement in trading receipts instead of being ambiguous. (i) Refuse `unless` and `requires` on every v2 rule at load, naming the rule, as D94 to D121 refuse forms whose effect would surprise. (ii) Keep D49's rule, an exception may exclude only wording on no list, and check it at load: a validator can test an exception against the names on other lists but not against what every other pattern can match, the D101 argument, and a table edit can always reach the next list's wording. (iii) Keep exceptions, but read them only after the verdict, to choose a category inside the bucket: one more tie-break inside a rule, where the category order already decides in the table (D61). *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D126), which R4 says since revision 14 (D131) and 4adddf9 builds; verify-67 found the same edit at 1b592b6. What (i) costs: a phrase that would have needed an exception goes on another list as a longer phrase, or the credit is ambiguous, as revision 6 said. No shipped verdict moves, since no shipped v2 rule has an exception, and v1 is untouched (R54).

**Sub-decision: cash wording inside a payment-method phrase (revision 13, after the owner's review-19 fix; D127, in R4 since revision 14).** R4 says a match inside a longer listed phrase counts only as the longer phrase, with one exception, the bank's code (D101). The owner's 1b592b6 adds a second: cash paid-in wording never counts as inside a payment-method cue's phrase, so with FPS CASH DEPOSIT on the rail list, FPS CASH DEPOSIT is ambiguous, rail and cash, not a rail alone. R4's text did not say so until revision 14, and R4's two clauses meet here as they did in D101: the longer-phrase clause hides the cash wording, while the clause on a payment-method cue with cash paid-in wording makes the credit ambiguous. (i) Write the exception into R4, as D101 did for the bank's code: cash paid-in wording is never taken in by a payment-method cue's longer phrase (what 1b592b6 builds). (ii) Refuse at load a payment-method name that holds wording on the cash list, and drop the exception: this works for names, not for patterns, which a table edit can use instead. (iii) Drop the exception and let the longer phrase win, as R4 read before revision 14: a rail phrase that holds cash wording then hides it, and a gateway beside it makes the credit trading receipts, which D52 forbids. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D127), which R4 says since revision 14 (D131). Its cost is a second exception to R4's longer-phrase clause, stated in R4. A payment rail and cash wording in one line are ambiguous however a table lists them, and an ambiguous credit is never trading receipts, so (i) can only lower M2 against (iii). The other side of the same meeting, a payment-method cue inside a cash phrase, is the next sub-decision.

**Sub-decision: a payment-method cue inside a cash phrase (revision 14, after 4adddf9; D132, in R4 since revision 15).** D127 keeps cash paid-in wording from being taken in by a payment-method cue's longer phrase. The other side was open: a table edit can list a cash phrase that holds payment-method wording, such as FPS CASH DEPOSIT or CHEQUES AND CASH PAID IN on the cash list. At 4adddf9 the rail or the cheque inside it then counts only as the cash phrase (src/cma/categorise.py:762-765), so FPS CASH DEPOSIT is a cash deposit, in trading receipts and M18, though the line says the money came by Faster Payment. R4's longer-phrase clause allows that reading, and its clause on a payment-method cue with cash paid-in wording forbids it. (i) Neither takes the other in: a payment-method cue and cash paid-in wording always meet at step 3, whichever list holds the longer phrase, so the credit is ambiguous; R4 says so in the next revision. (ii) The longer cash phrase takes the cue in, and R4 says so: the table author's phrase decides. (iii) Refuse at load a cash-list name that holds payment-method wording. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D132), which R4 says since revision 15 (D136) and 78556a7 builds; verify-69 found the edit at 4adddf9. (ii) lost because a table edit would then turn electronic money into cash takings in M2 without anyone seeing the choice, which R4's clause on a payment-method cue with cash paid-in wording exists to stop and which D52 ended for every other pair of lists. (iii) lost for D101's reason: it works for names, not for patterns, and a table edit can use a pattern. What (i) costs: one condition in T03's longer-phrase filter made two-way, and a counter deposit that a bank prints with a payment-method word inside cash wording waits in the queue. No shipped cash phrase holds payment-method wording, so no shipped verdict moves, and the step only ever makes a credit ambiguous, so it can lower M2, never raise it.

**Sub-decision: an exception written into a pattern (revision 15, after verify-69; D136, in R4 since revision 15).** D126 refuses an `unless` or a `requires` on a v2 rule. verify-69 wrote the same exception into the cash rule's pattern instead: KIOSK CASH DEPOSIT followed by a negative lookahead for STRIPE, with CASH DEPOSIT off the cash names. FPS KIOSK CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE then matched no cash wording, and the gateway made it a card settlement in trading receipts. (i) Refuse at load a v2 pattern with a lookahead or lookbehind, naming the rule, as D126 refuses the keys. (ii) Allow a lookaround whose body cannot match a letter, such as a test for a digit after INV, and refuse the rest: a body with no letter can still test that nothing or only a space follows, which hides wording as an anchor does (next sub-decision), and no shipped pattern needs one. (iii) Keep lookarounds, as v1 does: the hidden tie-break D52 ended. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D136), which 78556a7 builds. The whole-word test the matcher adds to every rule is the matcher's own, not the table's, and \b written in a pattern looks only at the character on each side of a match, as whole words do, so neither is an exception. What (i) costs: a table author who wants a phrase only where other wording is absent lists the longer phrase on the list it belongs to, or lets the line be ambiguous. No shipped v2 pattern has a lookaround, so no shipped verdict moves, and the frozen v1 table is untouched (R54).

**Sub-decision: anchors in a v2 pattern (revision 15, after 78556a7; D137, in R4 since revision 16).** A lookaround tests the text beside a match; an anchor tests the whole line beside it: ^ matches only where nothing comes before, $ only where nothing comes after. At 78556a7 a v2 pattern may carry one, since the validator tests lookarounds only (src/cma/categorise.py:433-439). With the cash rule's pattern written CASH DEPOSIT$, or ^CASH DEPOSIT, and CASH DEPOSIT off its names, FPS CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE matches no cash wording and is a card settlement in trading receipts: the edit verify-65 made with an `unless` and verify-69 with a lookahead. (i) Refuse at load a v2 pattern with an anchor (^, $, \A or \Z), naming the rule, as D136 refuses lookarounds. The bank's code listed in code keeps its ^, because the start of the line is the bank's own place (D97) and the validator already allows that pattern only on the flagged rule. R4 says so in the next revision. (ii) Allow anchors, and say in R4 that an anchored pattern lists wording only at the start or end of the line, so a line with that wording elsewhere does not hold it. (iii) Refuse only the end anchors ($, \Z), and keep ^ for wording a bank prints at the start of a line. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D137), which R4 says since revision 16 (D141) and 2c72469 builds. (ii) lost because a table edit could then switch any list's wording off wherever other wording precedes or follows it, the hidden tie-break D52, D126 and D136 ended, and R4's no-exception clause would need an exception of its own. (iii) lost because ^ hides wording as surely as $ does: ^CASH DEPOSIT misses FPS CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE just as CASH DEPOSIT$ does, and the one wording a bank prints at the start of a line that R4 gives standing, its returned-payment code, is listed in code already. What (i) costs: one validator check in T03 before its review, and a table author who wants wording only where a bank prints it at the start of a line lists it as a cue or a name, which match anywhere. The bank's code is the only anchored pattern v2 ships (src/cma/rules/v2.yaml:64), so no shipped verdict moves.

**Sub-decision: which matches can set another aside (revision 16, after verify-71; D141, in R4 since revision 16; its grain D142, in R4 since revision 17).** R4 says a match inside a longer listed phrase counts only as the longer phrase. Until 5c9a5bd every match set aside the matches inside it, even one that was itself set aside. verify-71 added FPS CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE to the cash list and FPS CASH DEPOSIT to the gateway list: the cash phrase set the gateway's match aside, the gateway's match still set aside the rail inside it, and FPS CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE became a cash deposit in trading receipts. (i) Any match sets aside what lies inside it (78556a7). (ii) Only a match whose rule counts sets others aside, with which rules count settled by repeating the filter over the rules that still count until it gives the same set, keeping the larger of two sets that alternate (5c9a5bd). (iii) Only a match that itself counts sets others aside, settled match by match from the longest down: a match counts unless it lies inside a longer match of another rule that counts, with the bank's code and the cash and payment-method pair exempt as before, and a rule counts where any match of its own counts. *Chose against (i)*, the owner's answer (D141), which R4 says since revision 16. *Between (ii) and (iii), chose (iii)*, the owner's answer (D142), which R4 says since revision 17 (D148) and 4b3b21c builds; verify-73 then failed 2c72469 on (ii)'s corner, with FPS CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE | STRIPE. (i) lost on verify-71. (ii) loses where a rule's other match props up a match that R4 sets aside. With FPS STRIPE CASH DEPOSIT on the rail list and STRIPE CASH DEPOSIT on the gateway list, FPS STRIPE CASH DEPOSIT REF STRIPE matches the gateway at STRIPE CASH DEPOSIT, inside the rail phrase, and at the trailing STRIPE, which nothing holds. The gateway counts by the trailing STRIPE, so at 2c72469 its first match still sets aside the cash wording inside it. The credit is then a card settlement in trading receipts, though it holds a rail and cash wording. Under (iii) that first match sets nothing aside, the cash wording counts, and the credit is ambiguous. (ii) also needs a rule, which R4 does not state, for two sets that alternate; under (iii) each match's standing rests only on longer matches, so there is one answer. What (iii) costs: credit_matches settles matches instead of rules. 4b3b21c repeats its filter over the matches until the set it keeps stops changing, rather than sorting them by length; since a match's standing rests only on strictly longer matches, the repetition reaches the one answer that settling from the longest down gives. The readings differ only where cash wording or a payment-method cue lies inside a third list's phrase that lies inside the other one's phrase, and no shipped phrase holds both, so no shipped verdict moves.

**Sub-decision: which table is spared the table checks (revision 18, after verify-75; D153, in R4 since revision 18; the exception check by its bytes, D168, in R4 since revision 21).** The frozen v1 table keeps its own rules, which carry no stage and an anchored bank code without the flag, and R54 keeps it as it is, so the checks the maintained lists get cannot apply to it. Until 9042fb4 the checks asked the table whether it was frozen. verify-75 set frozen: true on a copy of the v2 table whose cash rule's pattern was CASH DEPOSIT$: the anchor check was skipped, and the table was refused only because no hash is pinned for v2, in a message that named no rule. (i) Only a table that bears v1's id and calls itself frozen skips the checks, and its bytes must then match the hash pinned in code, or it is refused as a changed frozen table, naming the table; every other table gets every check, naming the rule (9042fb4). (ii) Run the checks on every table whose bytes are not v1's pinned bytes, a changed copy of v1 included: that copy would be refused for v1's own rules as well as for what changed, and the message that matters, reinstall the package, would sit among them. (iii) Keep trusting the flag, and have the refusal for a frozen table with no pinned hash run the checks too: two paths to one refusal, and the flag would still decide which checks a table gets. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D153), which R4 says since revision 18. What (i) costs: a changed copy of v1 is refused naming the table and its hash, not a rule, which R4 states; nothing loads from it, and reinstalling the package restores it. No shipped table moves. *The exception check by its bytes* (revision 21, after verify-81; D168): at 5139e22 a table loaded as v1 that called itself frozen skipped the exception check whatever its bytes, so a changed v1 that gained an unless was refused as changed with its unless unnamed. 5f5bb2e spares the exception check only for v1 as its bytes were verified, so a changed v1 names each rule that carries an exception after the changed-table refusal. (ii)'s cost stays avoided: a copy that still calls itself frozen is not held to the stages, the category order and the other checks only the maintained lists need, which v1's own rules lack. v1 carries no exception and only the bank's code as an anchor, so the check names only what a change adds. *Only by a digest that matches the pin* (revision 22, after verify-103; D192): at 5f5bb2e, and still at 792727b, the check of a table's data given no digest of its bytes took rules that bore v1's id and called themselves frozen as verified, and spared their exceptions. The loader always gives the digest, so loading held, but any other caller of the check spared whatever it was given. Since 4c017d4 only a digest that matches the pin spares a table, as R4 says since revision 22.

**Sub-decision: what a changed v1 is refused for first (revision 19, after verify-77; D158, in R4 and R54 since revision 19).** Since revision 18 only the frozen v1 table, known by the hash pinned in code, is spared the table checks. At 9042fb4 the loader parsed and checked a table loaded as v1 before it compared its bytes with the pin, so a copy of v1 that kept id: v1 and frozen: true with its rules list emptied was refused for having no rules, never as changed (verify-77). (i) Compare the bytes first; refuse any other bytes as a changed frozen table, naming the table, whatever the table says of itself, and name after that whatever else keeps it from loading (7cb2e71). (ii) Check the table first, as 9042fb4 did: a changed v1 that is also malformed is refused for the malformation, and the message that matters, reinstall the package, is lost. (iii) Compare the bytes only, and name nothing else: a person mending a hand-edited copy learns one fault at a time. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D158), which R4 and R54 say since revision 19. What (i) costs: a changed v1 is still parsed, to name what else it breaks; none of its rules is used. The same revision asks the validator to survive every value the format can hold: a value the format does not allow in a rule's field is named by rule, beside the table's other faults, never raised as a type error (R4 since revision 19). Revision 21 asks the same of a pattern that compiles alone but cannot be used in a form the matcher builds from it: every rule's matchers are built while the table is checked, so such a pattern is named beside the table's other faults, never found once they are mended (D168, after verify-81; 5f5bb2e).

**Sub-decision: what settles an ambiguous credit (revision 3; revised in revision 7, D78, D79; extended in revision 9, D86, D88).** (i) Only a per-line override. (ii) A per-line override, a case-profile name or an own-account pairing; never a firm rule (revisions 3 to 6, D54). (iii) Anything that ranks above the built-in table's unprotected rules, firm rules included. (iv) A per-line override or an own-account pairing; a case-profile name the wording matches adds its bucket as one more candidate; never a firm rule. *Chose (iv)*, the owner's answer (D78, D79). (ii) lost on verify-41: a profile name matches wherever its words occur in the description, and a bank line has no separate payer field, so a director named in a customer's payment reference settled FPS CREDIT ACME LTD REF CASH DEPOSIT FOR JANE DOE as director funding and dropped its candidates. Every bucket a profile name gives is non-trading, so M2 was never at risk, but a disagreement left the queue without a person seeing it, which is what D52 forbids. (i) lost because a pairing matches a debit and a credit of the same amount within days across the case's own accounts, not text in a reference, so no payer can fake it; refusing it would amend R8 and put both halves of every ambiguous own-account transfer in the queue. (iii) lost as before: a firm rule can name trading receipts, and it was previewed on another case's lines, not on this one. What (iv) costs: a director's or an own account's credit with ambiguous wording now waits in the queue for one override that revision 6 made for it; its candidates show the profile's bucket, so the override starts there. Since revision 9 the same holds for a credit that two kinds of profile name make ambiguous (D86, D88). Profile names still decide every credit that the table does not find ambiguous and only one kind of profile name matches (R5, R6), below a protected verdict as since revision 3, which R5 and R6 say since revision 8 (D81, D82).

**Sub-decision: where a protected verdict ranks against a case-profile name (revision 3; written into R5 and R6 in revision 8, D81, D82).** A line can carry both: REFUND JANE DOE, JANE DOE LOAN or IWOCA ACME HOLDINGS, with Jane Doe a director and ACME HOLDINGS an own account in the profile. (i) The protected verdict decides and the name is not read. (ii) The name decides, as R5 and R6 read before revision 8. (iii) The disagreement makes the line ambiguous, with the name as one more candidate. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D81). (ii) lost because a line-type cue or a lender's name says what the line is, while a profile name says only who is mentioned, payer or reference (D78): a refund that names the director would become director funding, and a drawdown from a named lender that mentions an own account would become a transfer, hiding new borrowing. (iii) lost because every protected verdict and every bucket a profile name gives are non-trading, so M2 is never at stake, and the queue would fill with refunds, loans and returned payments whose wording is not in doubt; it would also have to reach debits, which are never ambiguous. What (i) costs: a director's loan written with generic loan wording and the director's name (JANE DOE LOAN) counts as a lending drawdown (M21), not director funding (M20), until the underwriter moves it with an override; written DIRECTOR LOAN, it is director funding by the longer phrase. Since revision 9 a protected verdict also outranks two kinds of profile name: IWOCA JANE DOE ACME HOLDINGS is a lending drawdown, not ambiguous.

**Sub-decision: a credit two kinds of profile name claim (revision 9, D86, D88).** A credit can name both a director or shareholder and one of the applicant's own accounts: FPS CREDIT JANE DOE ACME HOLDINGS, with Jane Doe a director and ACME HOLDINGS an own account; or FPS CREDIT JANE DOE LTD, with Jane Doe a director and JANE DOE LTD an own account. (i) A fixed order between the kinds, director first (revisions 3 to 8, R5 before R6). (ii) The credit is ambiguous, with every table match and both kinds among its candidates, unless a protected verdict decides it; only an override or a pairing settles it. (iii) The longer or more specific name wins, as a longer listed phrase does in the table. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer. (i) lost for the reason D52 gave for the table: an order between two lists is a choice nobody sees, and the description cannot say whether the director paid, or the own account, or neither (D78). (iii) lost because a longer name is no better evidence of the payer. JANE DOE LTD as an own account says only that the company is mentioned, and a containment rule between profile names would be a tie-break no person sees. What (ii) costs: one override for each such credit. A director's name inside an own account's name queues every credit naming that account. A name the profile lists twice, such as a holding company entered as a shareholder and as an own account, sends every credit naming it to the queue until the profile is corrected. Both buckets are non-trading, so M2 never depended on the order; the change only stops the tool choosing between them. A protected verdict still decides first (R5, R6), and a debit, never ambiguous, still takes an own account's name (R6).

**Sub-decision: what an ambiguous credit records as its candidates (D60, revision 4; widened in revision 7; revision 9, D90).** (i) The matches of the stage that decided (revision 3's reading, and T03's fifth attempt). (ii) Every rule the credit matched in either stage, after a match inside a longer phrase counts as the longer one, payment-method cues included (the rail, and since revision 6 a cheque). Since revision 7, also each kind of case-profile name its wording matches (D78). Since revision 9, the same list for a credit that only two profile kinds make ambiguous. (iii) For a credit only the profile makes ambiguous, the rule that would have decided and the profile kinds, or the profile kinds alone. *Chose (ii)*: the owner's answer against (i) (D60) and against (iii) (D90). (i) lost because the underwriter would not see a name that the cues outranked: FPS CREDIT HMRC TO SAVINGS STRIPE would show HMRC and savings and hide the gateway, which is the reason a person might call it trading. A profile candidate likewise shows the reading revision 6 would have chosen without asking. (iii) lost for the same reason: FPS CREDIT STRIPE INV 17 JANE DOE ACME HOLDINGS would show the customer transfer and the two profile names and hide the gateway, and one term would mean two things depending on what made the credit ambiguous. What (ii) costs: longer lists, and a payment-method candidate that gives no bucket, which the queue shows as such.

**Sub-decision: where the judgement queue is required (D60, revision 4; revision 9, D91).** (i) In R4, so the categoriser's task also had to show the queue (revision 3). (ii) In R15, the case view, whose task builds the queue; R4 requires only what the categoriser produces. (iii) In both R4 and R15. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer. (i) lost because T03 could not complete R4 without the workspace, which depends on T03 (the fifth review failed it for that). (iii) lost for the same reason. Revision 9 keeps the split for R5 and R6. They mark a credit both profile kinds claim, place it in unexplained money in with its candidates, and say R15 lists it. T05, which builds R5 and R6, depends on T19 and T03, not on T21, which builds the queue (D91). What (ii) leaves out: `cma run` is not a case and shows no queue; its evidence lines carry the conflict and candidates (Out of scope).

**Sub-decision: which category a credit gets inside one bucket (revision 4).** Two rules can agree on the bucket but carry different categories: a gateway (card settlement) and an invoice reference (customer transfer) are both trading receipts; a returned-payment cue and a refund cue are both refunds and returned payments. The category matters downstream: the round-trip check undoes only customer transfers, M15 reads customer transfers, M7, reversal linking and the returned-DD indicator read returned payments, the M16 numerator reads refunds, and M18 and the cash indicators read cash deposits. (i) The first rule in table order (revision 3): reordering rules then changes M2, M7 and M15, which the fifth review showed. (ii) Make it ambiguous: R4 defines ambiguity by bucket, so this would amend R4 and fill the queue with credits whose bucket is not in doubt. (iii) A category order declared with the table and applied once the bucket is known, putting first the category that brings more checks. *Chose (iii)*, which the owner confirmed (D61): in trading receipts, cash deposit, then customer transfer, then card settlement; in refunds and returned payments, returned payment, then refund. A payer cannot avoid the round-trip check by adding a gateway's name, or the cash flags by adding an invoice reference. What (iii) costs: a gateway payout that carries an invoice reference counts as a named customer in M15. The order is data in the table and is hashed with it.

**Sub-decision: how far a listed name stretches (D71, D72, revision 6).** (i) The spelling on the list, with any run of spaces between its words (revision 5). (ii) Any run of separators between any two characters of a listed name or a profile name: whitespace, dot, slash, the minus sign, every Unicode dash and every invisible format character; nothing else. (iii) Approximate matching (edit distance, sound-alike codes). *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer. (i) lost because bank text spells one name many ways: FASTER-PAYMENT and FASTER–PAYMENT hid the rail and counted a credit as cash (verify-38, verify-39), and SUM UP CASH ADVANCE missed the lender phrase spelt SUMUP and counted as card takings. (iii) lost because a near match is a guess: it can make a payer's name look like an acquirer's, and nobody could say why a line matched. What (ii) costs: a name can match where its letters are split across words, so a short listed name can match initials or two short words. S O SMITH reads as a standing order, which can only make cash wording ambiguous; E BAY reads as eBay and counts as trading receipts, which the irregular-acquirer indicator reads. A listed name or profile value made only of separators names no one and matches nothing (D72).
