**Sub-decision: how far the case log's concurrency goes (D63, D65, revision 5).** (i) Support every way a process could share a case: forked children, network shares, any mix of handles (T12's attempts before D63, with fork hooks and per-acquisition tokens). (ii) State a scope and refuse what falls outside it: one writer at a time per case across windows, commands, threads and separate CaseLog objects in one process, on a local disk, with no fork while the lock is held. (iii) Hand out one shared CaseLog per case in each process, so separate objects never meet. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer. (i) lost because the tool never forks, yet the fork machinery cost three review rounds, and the fifth review's first finding was a race inside the fork hooks themselves. (iii) lost because it holds only if every caller goes through the shared handle: a test or a later command that builds its own CaseLog would bypass it, while the operating-system lock separates handles whichever code built them, on a local disk. What (ii) gives up: on a network share the operating system may emulate the lock per process, so two CaseLog objects in one process might both write, and nothing detects a share (D66: stated, not detected). A workspace that forked worker processes would also fall outside the scope, so C5 serves from one process.

**Sub-decision: what a reader may report while another writer holds the case (D65, revision 5; D74, revision 6; revision 7 after verify-42; revision 9 after verify-46; revision 10, D95).** A writer adds its entry to the log and then records it in the head record; a reader that looks in between sees more than the recorded entries, and cannot tell a live append from an entry a crash left. (i) Report what it reads: every reader calls that entry incomplete, so a second window warns of a broken log whenever the first one saves, and asks the underwriter to drop a live append. (ii) Excuse an append in progress (revision 5, D67): only a holder that is appending excuses what follows the recorded entries, which the holder marks in the lock file (D69). (iii) Claim less (D74): while another writer holds the case, judge only the recorded entries, from a read across which the head record did not change; report anything wrong at or before them as broken, and the rest as being saved, never intact and never broken; with no such read within the retries, report the case as being saved and claim nothing. The holder and any reader of a free case judge the whole log. (iv) Wait for the lock before reporting: never a wrong reading, but every window stalls behind a writer, and a stuck writer stalls them all. *Chose (iii)*, the owner's answer. (ii) lost because telling an appending holder from a checking one needed writer state that the reader reads at a different moment from the log, and each review found a race in it: a holder that only checks made a stale torn entry look live (verify-36); the mark could not tell one append from the next, a live repair read as incomplete, and exhausted retries hid a removed entry (verify-40). (i) and (iv) lost as in revision 5. Revision 7 closed a gap verify-42 found: a reader tries the lock first, so it learns whether the case is free before it says anything about the whole log; it differs from (iv) in that it never waits for a writer that holds the case. Revision 9 closed the matching gap at the other end: a holder can let go between the reader's probe and its read, so being saved stands only if a writer still holds the case after the read. Revision 10 takes in how the reader now knows that the writer it found was the same one throughout: the gate (next sub-decision). What (iii) gives up: while another writer holds the case, a reader says nothing about anything after the recorded entries and never says intact. A torn entry a crash left is reported only when the holder looks or the case is free. Under a stream of saves from one window a reader may claim nothing at all, and `cma case check` says to look again. Neither lets a bad log through a sign-off or an append, which judge the whole log while holding the case.

**Sub-decision: how a reader knows one writer held the case through its read (D93, D95, D98; revision 10).** (i) Try the case lock before the read and again after it (revision 9, D87): verify-47 showed the two tries can see two different writers' holds, with the case free and an incomplete entry between them. (ii) Writers count their holds in the lock file, and the reader compares counts (D93): verify-49 showed a writer takes the lock before it raises the count, so two holds can share one count, and the lock file held writer state again, the thing revision 6 removed. (iii) A gate: a writer passes it alone and briefly to take the case; a reader holds it shared through its read, so no writer can take the case during a read, and a writer that holds the case when the reader looks holds it throughout, until it lets go (D95). (iv) Wait for the lock before reading: lost in revision 5. *Chose (iii)*, the owner's take-over. It needs no writer state: the operating system's locks say everything, and the lock files hold no data. What (iii) costs: a second lock file in every case folder; a writer waits at the gate while checks hold it, up to a set time, so a check run in a tight loop can hold off a save until it is refused; and a writer paused while it holds the gate has not taken the case, so a check that finds the gate busy tries the case lock directly and reads holding it if it is free (D98, after verify-52). If the case is held as well, the check does not hold the gate, and the next sub-decision says what it may report.

**Sub-decision: a writer at the gate while another holds the case (revision 11, after verify-54 and verify-55; D109, in R43 and P15 since revision 12).** A check that finds the gate busy tries the case lock directly (D98). If the case lock is held too, the check does not hold the gate, so it cannot know that one writer held the case through its read. The holder can let go, leaving an incomplete entry, and the writer at the gate can take the case between the check's two looks. That is verify-47's corner, which the gate closes only for a check that holds it. (i) From a steady read, name a change, removal or reordering at or before the recorded entries, which is wrong whoever holds the case. Otherwise try again, and when the retries run out report being saved and claim nothing (D105; what T12 builds). R43 and P15 then say that a steady read gives the recorded entries only when one writer held the case through it. (ii) From a steady read, report the recorded entries and the rest as being saved, as verify-55 read R43 as revision 11 wrote it; no requirement change. (iii) Wait at the gate for the writer passing it before reading. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D109), which R43 and P15 say since revision 12 (D112). (ii) lost because it reopens verify-47's corner. A check would report being saved over an incomplete entry left during a free moment in its read: it would say a save is under way when the entry is a crash's, which R43 asks a reader that finds the case free to name. An adversarial review would build that schedule. (iii) is what the retries already do, pausing between attempts; a writer paused at the gate through all of them still leaves the choice between (i) and (ii). What (i) costs: a check that meets a writer paused at the gate while another holds the case either names a fault at or before the recorded entries or says only that the case is being saved.

**Sub-decision: lock files removed or replaced while a case is in use (D98, D99; revision 10).** verify-52 showed a writer that had checked its lock file could still append after someone replaced the file and a second writer had taken the new one. (i) Detect and refuse every interference, by joining the check of the lock file to each write: no file system offers that as one step, so a window remains between the check and the write, and each review would find it again. (ii) State it: the gate and the case lock are the tool's own files; removing or replacing them while the case is in use is outside R42's guarantee, as hand-editing the log is. A writer re-checks its lock before each write and refuses if the file was removed or replaced, and if such interference still lets two writers append, the log they leave is reported broken, never intact. (iii) Lock the log file itself, so that removing the lock removes the log: the repair replaces the log by rename, which would hand its lock to nobody, and a reader's gate would still need its own file. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D99). Two appends at one position leave two entries that claim it, so the chain or the head record fails there whatever order their writes take. What (ii) gives up: a cleanup script, a sync or backup client restoring a folder, or a person deleting the lock files can let two writers append; the log is then reported broken, and the second save is redone after the log is repaired. A repair is covered only by the sub-decision after next.

**Sub-decision: which steps a writer re-checks its lock before (revision 11, after verify-54 and verify-55; D108, in R42 and P15 since revision 12).** R42 says a writer re-checks its lock before each write. An append changes the case's files in up to three steps: the empty head record before the first line, the line, and the rename that publishes the new head record. A repair has two: the rename of the rewritten log over the old one, and the rename of the head record. Each rename is prepared in a temporary file that nobody reads. (i) Before each step that changes a file a reader reads: the empty head record, the line and each rename; writing a temporary file is not such a step. (ii) Before each append and each repair, as revision 10's C3 said, and amend R42 to say so. (iii) Make the check and the step one: no file system offers it (revision 10). *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D108), which R42 and P15 name since revision 12 (D112). It is what R42's words say and what verify-55 asks, and each check is one file status call. It leaves only the window between a check and the step itself, which revision 10 states. (ii) lost because it keeps a wider window than R42's text allows, so R42 would have to change for a weaker guarantee. What (i) costs: a step refused after the line and before the head record leaves the entry past the recorded entries, as a crash does. The writer says the save was not recorded, and the next holder confirms dropping it.

**Sub-decision: a repair under such interference (revision 10; D103; narrowed in revision 13, D124).** Dropping a torn entry rewrites the log through a temporary file and a rename, so that a crash leaves the old log or the repaired one. Suppose interference lets a second writer take the case, drop the same torn entry and append, all between the first writer's re-check and its rename. The rename then removes the second writer's entries, and the first writer's head record matches what is left, so the log reads intact. (i) State it: under such interference a repair can remove another writer's entries without a break, as a hand edit of the log and its head record together can; the files cannot show it, and a signed pack pins the head it saw. (ii) Make the repair an append: the torn bytes stay, ended by a newline, followed by a drop entry that names them, and a reader skips a line only when the entry after it names it. Every write is then an append, so D99's 'broken, never intact' covers repairs too. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D103), as D63 and D66 kept to stating a scope. (ii) lost for now because it changes the log format and the reader in a task that has used its attempts, for a case that needs both interference and a torn entry. What (i) costs: D99's guarantee holds for appends; a repair under interference can lose another writer's save silently until a signed pack is compared. Revision 13 narrows (i) (D124). verify-64's schedule was within it: A, refused after its line, had not been told its save was recorded when B's confirmed drop removed its entry, so no recorded save was lost. R42 now says so, and says the rest: two saves told recorded at one log position leave a broken log, and under such interference a log that reads intact holds every save told recorded. A repair may therefore remove only the entry of a save told it was not recorded, and the sub-decision after next says how a writer knows which it was told.

**Sub-decision: one save at a time per holder (revision 13, D124).** verify-64's first major. The writer lock belongs to a thread and nests, so that a sign-off can hold the case across its check and its append. A save begun inside another on the same thread, from a signal handler, a finaliser or a callback, was admitted, and both committed at the position the outer save had checked. (i) A save-in-progress mark per holder: a save begun while one is under way on the same thread is refused before it writes anything; and an append refuses if the log grew since its check, whatever added the line (b428d56). (ii) Make the writer lock not nest: a sign-off would have to let the case go between its check and its append, which reopens the gap the nesting closes. (iii) The growth check alone: the inner save commits and the outer one is refused, so the save the person started is the one lost. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D124), which R42 and P15 state since revision 13. What (i) costs: a callback that saves from inside a save is refused and has to save again afterwards; nothing in the tool does that today.

**Sub-decision: when a writer is told its save was recorded (revision 13; D125, in R42 since revision 14).** R42 says that under lock-file interference a log that reads intact holds every save whose writer was told it was recorded. At b428d56 a save is told recorded as soon as the rename of its head record returns, and a repair renames the log and then its head record with only the lock re-checked before each. Revision 13 found a schedule that defeats this (Context). A's re-check before its head rename passes; the lock file is replaced; B takes the new lock, drops A's line as torn and renames the repaired log; A's head record lands, and A is told recorded; B's head record lands over A's. The log and B's head record agree, so the log reads intact, without A's save. (i) Tell a save recorded when its head record's rename returns, and write into R42 that a repair whose check came before another save's head record was published may remove that save's entry. (ii) Tell a save recorded only after it reads back, once its head record is published, that the log holds its entry where that head record lists it, and otherwise report it as not recorded; and have a repair re-check, immediately before each of its renames, that the log and the head record are still as its check read them, refusing otherwise. (iii) Make every write an append, so a repair never rewrites the log (D103's option (ii)): the head record is still one file that the last rename sets, so B's head record still drops A's entry. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D125), which R42 says since revision 14 (D131) and 1d16873 builds; verify-66 then found two schedules of this kind at b428d56. A save whose head record lands after a repair's log rename finds its entry gone and is told not recorded. A repair that checked before a save's head record landed finds the head record changed at its next rename and refuses, and the log is then broken, never intact. Each check leaves a window before its step, as revision 10 says, but the two checks cover each other's: for A to be told recorded and still lose its entry, B's log rename must follow A's read-back, and then B's re-check before its head rename finds A's head record. R42 names the repair's re-check rename by rename. Before the log rename it re-checks the log and the head record. Before the head rename it re-checks the head record, because the log is then the one the repair's own rename published, and the repair's read-back checks it. 1d16873 also has every save publish its head record only if the head record still lists what its check read, which the read-back would catch anyway. (i) lost because it gives up the guarantee D124 asks for, and T12 would be judged against R42's text. What (ii) costs: two reads after each save, and a repair refused after its log rename leaves a broken log to resolve by hand; both happen only under interference.

**Sub-decision: which lock files a writer re-checks (revision 15, after verify-68; D136, in R42 since revision 15).** R42 said a writer re-checks its lock before each step a reader reads, and 1d16873 re-checked the case lock only; verify-68 replaced the gate during a save, and nothing stopped it. A reader learns from the gate whether a writer has started and whether one held the case through its read. A reader that finds no gate takes the case as one no writer has started, and one that holds a new gate takes the writer it finds holding the case to have passed it. (i) Re-check both lock files before each step: the gate's identity, noted when the writer passed it, and the case lock's (cd298cc). (ii) Re-check the case lock only, and state in R42 that a removed or replaced gate is outside the guarantee with nothing to stop the writer: a reader that then finds no gate would judge a steady read as the whole log while the writer adds to it, naming a live append incomplete or calling the log intact. (iii) Hold the gate through the whole save, so that it cannot change unnoticed: every check would then wait for every save, which revision 10 rejected when it made the gate a brief pass. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D136), which R42 says since revision 15 and cd298cc builds. What (i) costs: one more file status call per check, and a writer that holds the case refuses its next step when only the gate is replaced, though its case lock is still its own; both happen only under interference.

**Sub-decision: how the first empty head record is published (revision 15, after verify-68).** The first append writes an empty head record before its line (revision 9, D87), so that a missing head record beside any entry is damage. 1d16873 published it like every head record, by renaming a staged file into place. verify-68 paused a first append just before that rename. With the case lock file replaced, B took the new lock, appended entry 1 and was told it was recorded. A's rename then put an empty head record over B's, B's entry became a torn write past the recorded entries, and a confirmed repair left a log that read intact without a save told recorded, which R42 rules out since revision 13. (i) Create the empty head record only where none exists: link the staged file into place, which fails if a head record is there, and stop with a conflict (cd298cc). (ii) Rename as before, after re-checking that no head record exists: the check and the rename are two steps, and verify-68's schedule fits between them. (iii) Create the head record exclusively and write it in place: a reader could find the file created but still empty, which reads as a damaged head record, not an empty one. *Chose (i)*, which the owner built in cd298cc (D134). R42's text already covers it, so no requirement changes. It closes the window rather than narrowing it, because the link itself refuses. What (i) costs: a file system with hard links. NTFS, APFS and the usual Linux file systems have them; FAT and exFAT, common on USB sticks, do not, and there the first append fails with the file system's error and writes nothing. The README and the trial sheet name the file systems to use.

**Sub-decision: a read across a lock file that changed (revision 16, after verify-70; D141, in R43 and P15 since revision 16).** A reader learns from the gate it holds that no writer can take the case during its read, and from the case lock whether one holds it (revision 10). verify-70 replaced the gate during a reader's steady read: the writer that held the case let go, a second writer took the same case lock through the new gate, and both of the reader's looks at the case lock found it held, so it reported the recorded entries and the rest as being saved though no one writer held the case through its read. (i) Trust the lock files as the reader took them (cd298cc). (ii) After each read, compare each lock file the reader held through it with what the path names now, and take the read again if one was removed or replaced, claiming nothing from that read (f5436bb). (iii) Report the case as being saved whenever a held lock file changed. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D141), which R43 and P15 say since revision 16. (i) lost on verify-70. (iii) lost because it says a save is under way where the reader cannot know one is, which is verify-70's fault in other words, and R43 asks a reader that cannot know to claim nothing. The reader holds each file it compares, so a replacement at any moment before the comparison shows there. What (ii) costs: one file status call per lock file per read, and under lasting interference a check that runs out of retries and claims nothing. At f5436bb the read a check takes holding the case lock alone, after finding the gate busy, was not compared (src/cma/case.py:527-533), nor was a holder's own read; verify-72 failed both, and 0d339cc compares both (R43 since revision 17).

**Sub-decision: a save whose read-back fails (revision 16, after verify-70; D141, in R42 since revision 16).** A save is told recorded only after reading back an intact log that holds its entry (D125). verify-70 failed that read-back with a one-shot read error: the save was told it was not recorded while its head record still listed its entry, so a fresh check read the log intact with the entry, and the next holder had no torn entry to drop, where P15 says a save refused after its line leaves its entry past the recorded entries. (i) Leave the head record as published (cd298cc). (ii) Take back the head record the save published, when it is still the save's own, by putting back the head record its check read, through a head rename re-checked as every head rename is (f5436bb). (iii) Read back again until a read succeeds. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D141), which R42 says since revision 16. (i) lost on verify-70. (iii) lost because a lasting error would hold the save, and a read-back that finds the entry gone, after a repair under interference, would still leave the save's head record listing it. A head record another writer has published since is left alone, since putting back the old one would remove that writer's save. What (ii) costs: one more rename when a read-back fails, and a take-back that can itself fail, which the next two sub-decisions take up.

**Sub-decision: a take-back that cannot be made (revision 16; D143, in R42 and P15 since revision 17).** The take-back is a head rename, so it re-checks both lock files first (R42), and it can fail: the head record cannot be read or renamed, or a lock file was replaced. At f5436bb the save then says it was not recorded and that its head record could not be taken back, and asks for the case log to be checked before anything else is saved (src/cma/case.py:1004-1011). Its entry stays among the recorded entries, so the next check reads the log intact with it. (i) Report the save as not confirmed, neither recorded nor not recorded, and ask for the case log to be checked, which shows whether the entry is there; R42 and P15 name that third answer in the next revision. (ii) Keep 'not recorded', and write into R42 and P15 that such a save's entry can stay among the recorded entries. (iii) Retry the take-back until it is made or the head record is no longer the save's own. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D143), which R42 and P15 say since revision 17 (D148) and 3076547 builds as SaveNotConfirmed. (ii) lost because the tool would tell a person a save was not recorded while the log holds it, and a person who saves again records the change twice. (iii) lost because a lasting error, a full disk or a lost mount, would hold the window. What (i) costs: a third answer for a save, in R42 and P15, the workspace message and T12's exception; it happens only when a read-back and its take-back both fail.

**Sub-decision: what a take-back checks before its rename (revision 17, after verify-72; D148 asked for the head record alone; D149, in R42 since revision 17).** The take-back is a head rename (revision 16). verify-72 replaced the gate after a save published its head record and failed the read-back with a one-shot read error. f5436bb's take-back re-checked the lock files and refused, and the save said it was not recorded while its head record listed its entry. (i) Re-check both lock files and the head record before the take-back's rename, as before every head rename, and answer not confirmed when either check stops it (D143); verify-72's schedule then ends not confirmed. (ii) Check only that the head record is still the save's own, whatever the lock files, as 0d339cc builds and D148 asks R42 to say. (iii) Make no take-back, and answer not confirmed whenever a read-back fails. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D149), which R42 has said since revision 17 and 7c8fba2 builds; verify-74 then failed 3076547, which checked the head record alone, on (ii)'s schedule. (ii) loses to the schedule in Context (T12's twenty-fourth review). A repair that checked before the save's head record landed publishes its own head record after the take-back's check and is told recorded; the take-back then lands over it, and a later confirmed drop leaves an intact log without that repair's save. R42 rules that out, so (ii) would need a second exception in R42's guarantee, beside D124's. Under (i) the repair holds the case only through a replaced case lock, which the take-back's re-check finds. A writer that takes the case after that re-check checked a head record that lists the save's entry, so its own entry follows that entry. A take-back that then lands over its head record leaves two or more entries past the recorded ones, and the log reads broken, never intact. (iii) lost because a read error alone, with no interference, would leave the save not confirmed where a take-back makes it plainly not recorded. What (i) costs: under interference, a failed read-back after a lock file was replaced answers not confirmed, and the person checks the case log before saving again. 7c8fba2 turns the lock re-check back on for the take-back, and the test at tests/test_case_log.py:2401 now expects not confirmed.

**Sub-decision: a writer that takes the case through a gate replaced before the read (revision 16; D144, in R43 and P15 since revision 17).** A writer passes the gate, checks that the gate is still the case's, and then takes the case lock (src/cma/case.py:704-714 at f5436bb). Paused between the two, it can take the case lock after the gate file is replaced, through a gate no reader holds. Each later step it tries refuses, since the gate it passed is gone (R42), but the case shows as held. A reader that holds the new gate and finds the case held by the writer before it may then judge a read across which that writer let go, leaving an incomplete entry, and report being saved; its own lock files stay the case's, so revision 16's comparison passes. (i) State in R43 and P15 that, beyond a read across a lock file the reader holds, lock-file interference is outside what a reader can know, and that a check after it stops gives the true answer. (ii) Also have a writer re-check, once it holds the case lock, that the gate it passed is still the case's, and let the case go if not: this narrows the moment without closing it, since a reader can look while the writer holds the lock. (iii) Leave R43 and P15 as revision 16 writes them. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D144), which R43 and P15 say since revision 17 (D148). (iii) lost because R43 and P15 say when a reader may report being saved without saying that lock-file interference bounds it, and T12's reviewer judges P15 by its own text. (ii) is not chosen alone because it leaves the same statement to make. What (i) costs: one sentence in R43 and P15; no build change.

**Sub-decision: what a save answers when the file system fails one of its steps (revision 18, after verify-74; D153, in R42 since revision 18; its reach D154, in R42 since revision 19, D158; the reads a save makes only to check, D159, in R42 since revision 20, D163; the answer known from the rename, D168, in R42 since revision 21).** After its check a save reads, writes, syncs, links and renames files, and the file system can fail any of these: a full disk, an I/O error, a file system without hard links. verify-74 failed the folder sync just after a head record's rename. The head record already listed the save, and the caller got the operating system's error, which is none of R42's answers. (i) Answer by where the failure falls: before the rename that publishes the save's head record, not recorded, since the head record does not list the entry; after it, when the head record cannot be made durable, not confirmed, since the head record lists the entry but a power cut could still undo the rename. Either answer names the error, and the error never reaches the caller as itself. (ii) Take back the head record when its folder cannot be synced, as after a failed read-back: the take-back's rename needs the same sync to last, so it cannot make the answer surer. (iii) Let the operating system's error through, and document that the caller checks the case log. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D153), which R42 says since revision 18; 7c8fba2 builds it where the head record is published (_publish, src/cma/case.py:1031-1041), telling the two answers apart by whether the head record still lists what the save's check read. (iii) lost because a caller that hears an error it cannot place may save again over a save the log holds, and the workspace would have to turn each error into a message on its own. (ii) lost for the reason given. What (i) costs: after a failed sync the person checks the case log before saving again, and a check made before a power cut can show the entry recorded where one made after it shows a torn write; both are rare, and the check gives the log's answer at that moment. *Its reach* (D154; R42 since revision 19, D158): every step a save takes after its check, the owner's answer to revision 18's question. 51c8b68 maps the steps that change a file, the first empty head record, the log's open, the line's write and sync, the folder sync after a new log and a repair's read, staging file, log rename and folder sync, to not recorded, naming the error. Revision 19 writes the answer as one rule: not recorded, or not confirmed when the save's head record was published and not taken back. That reads D158's 'except where the head record was published' as a head record that still lists the save's entry when the save answers, since a save whose take-back is made is not recorded (R42 since revision 16). Taking the case comes before a save's check, so a lock that cannot be taken is as R43 says: the append fails with the lock's error and writes nothing. *The reads a save makes only to check (asked in revision 19; D159, read by read in R42 since revision 20, D163):* a save also reads in order to check: it compares the log's bytes and the head record with its check before its line and its renames, examines both lock files before each step, and reads itself back. At 51c8b68 each of those reads turns the file system's error into a value: an unreadable log reads as changed, an unreadable head record as changed, a lock file that cannot be examined as removed or replaced. The answer then names no error, and before the line it is a conflict that asks the person to reload (Context, T12's twenty-sixth review). (a) Count them among R42's steps: every answer names the error, and a re-check that cannot read what it checks answers not recorded, never a conflict; R42 says so since revision 19 (design default). (b) Keep R42's naming to the steps that change a file, and say in R42 that a re-check or a read-back that cannot read what it reads answers as one that finds a change: a conflict before the line, not recorded after it, and the take-back rules after the head record's rename, naming no error. *Chose (a)*, the owner's answer (D159), which R42 names read by read since revision 20 (D163) and 1e35352 builds, because D158 says every step, R42 lists reading among a save's steps, and a person told to reload a case whose log cannot be read reloads into the same error. What (a) costs: T12 carries the error out of six reads before its review. (b) costs no build change and leaves a disk error answered as somebody else's save. *Known from the rename* (revision 21, after verify-80; D168): (i) tells the two answers apart by where the failure falls, before or after the head record's rename. 7c8fba2 decided it by reading the head record back, and when that read failed too, as when the re-check before the rename failed for that very reason, it answered not confirmed though no rename was made (verify-80). Since 26bc51e the save notes when its rename is done and answers from that, never from a head record it may not be able to read, as R42 says since revision 21.

**Sub-decision: what a save refused partway through its line answers (revision 19, after verify-76; D158, in R42 and P15 since revision 19).** A writer re-checks both lock files before every write call of its line (D116), so a short write continued in a second call can be refused with part of the line already on disk. At 7c8fba2 that refusal said nothing was written, while the next check named the part as an incomplete entry (src/cma/case.py:1010 there). (i) Not recorded, never nothing written: the part stays past the recorded entries as a torn entry, as a crash leaves it, for the next holder's repair (9c265a3, which tells the two apart by whether the log is longer than the save's check found it). (ii) Cut the log back to the length the check found, then answer nothing written: that changes a file a reader reads after the writer lost its lock, which R42 refuses. (iii) Keep the message, and let the next check name the torn entry: the person is told nothing was written about a case that now needs a repair before anything else is saved. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D158), which R42 and P15 say since revision 19. What (i) costs: nothing beyond the message; the torn entry is what a crash at that moment leaves, and the existing repair drops it. Revision 20 extends (i) to every part of a save's report (D163, after verify-78; R42 and P15). At 51c8b68 a save refused at its head rename, after the whole line, was told it was not recorded in words that ended with the lock check's own, 'nothing was written'; de6a56e words that refusal for the save. A repair's line is in the log once its repaired log is renamed over the log. A repair refused before that rename leaves the case's files as they were, and 'nothing was written' is true of it. Revision 21 carries (i) into the report's causes: the words of any refusal or error a report is raised from are part of it (D168, after verify-80). 26bc51e words the lock check's own refusal as the step refused, so no cause a save carries says nothing was written; a save refused before any part of its line is in the log should still be told it was not recorded (Context, T12's twenty-eighth review).

**Sub-decision: what a not-confirmed report may quote (revision 20, after the owner's 1e35352; D164, in R42 since revision 21, D168).** A save whose read-back fails and whose take-back is refused is not confirmed (D143). At 1e35352 its report says so, and quotes in brackets the refusal that stopped the take-back (src/cma/case.py:1100-1104). Two refusals can stand there. A replaced case lock file gives the lock check's refusal, which ends 'nothing was written' (:807-808), though the save's line is in the log. R42 and P15 rule that out since revision 20, so T12 changes it before its review whichever option is chosen. A head record no longer the save's own gives the head check's refusal, which ends 'the save was not recorded' (:1002-1003), inside a report that R42 calls neither recorded nor not recorded. (i) One answer per report. The take-back's refusal is worded for the save, naming what stopped it: a lock file removed or replaced, a head record another writer published, or the error met. No part of a not-confirmed report says that the save was recorded, was not recorded or wrote nothing. R42 says so in the next revision (design default). (ii) A not-confirmed report may quote what stopped its take-back in that refusal's own words, and only 'nothing was written' is ruled out; R42 says so in the next revision. (iii) Name no cause, and say only that the save is not confirmed. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D164), which R42 says since revision 21 (D168) and 7fb3a23 builds. A person who reads 'the save was not recorded' inside a not-confirmed report may save again over a save the log holds, which is what D143 ended. T12's reviewer also reads R42's 'neither recorded nor not recorded' at its finest grain, as verify-78 read 'never as nothing written'. (ii) keeps that risk for the sake of a quotation. (iii) loses what the person should look for before saving again: a lock file a cleanup tool touched, or another window's save. What (i) costs: T12 words two refusals for the take-back, as de6a56e did for the head rename, and its test at tests/test_case_log.py:2401 asserts the words as well as the class. D168 adds the report's causes: 7fb3a23 neither quotes nor chains the take-back's refusal, and 26bc51e words the lock check's own refusal as the step refused, so no refusal or error a save's report carries says nothing was written (tests/test_case_log.py:2601, :2650).

**Sub-decision: a busy-gate read whose case lock is removed during it (revision 18, after verify-74; D153, in R43 since revision 18).** A check that finds the gate busy holds the case lock shared and reads, and its read counts only while that case lock is still the case's (R43 since revision 17). verify-74 removed the case lock file, rather than replacing it, between the read and the comparison. The comparison raised the error a missing file raises, which 3076547's reader took for the absence of a gate, so it trusted a steady read that no lock file kept writers out of, and a writer that created and took a new case lock meanwhile could have its live append reported torn. (i) A lock file missing at the comparison was removed during the read: the reader looks again through the gate and the case lock, as at its first attempt (7c8fba2). (ii) Treat the missing case lock as a case no writer holds and judge the read, as a reader behind the gate with no case lock file does: that reader holds the gate, which keeps writers out, and this one does not. (iii) Report the case as being saved: the reader cannot know that a save is under way. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D153), which R43 says since revision 18 for every read taken again. What (i) costs: nothing beyond the attempts the retries already allow; with every attempt so, the check claims nothing, as R43 says.

**Sub-decision: what a reader's check of its lock files can see (revision 22, after verify-96 to verify-102; D192, in R43 and P15 since revision 22).** A reader's read counts only while the lock files it counts on are still the case's (revision 16), so after its read it looks at them. Each look compares one file with what its path names, and the looks come one after another, so whichever file was looked at first can be replaced during the look at the other. verify-96 and verify-98 placed the replacement there in turn, first after the case lock's look and then after the gate's. verify-100 failed a holder's read that stood on its case lock alone. Once 57984ea also tested whether each held file still had a name, verify-102 moved the gate to another name, which keeps it one, and found an ordinary reader that looked at the gate only before the case lock. (i) Look at each file in turn and then the first again, and, for a holder, also test that each file held still has a name. State in R43 and P15 that a lock file removed or replaced after the check's last look at it is outside what any check can know, as D99 states it for writers (523c5df). (ii) Keep looking until two whole passes agree. A replacement after the last look of the last pass is still unseen, so the window moves and does not close, and under a stream of interference the check never ends. (iii) Hold a lock that keeps every lock file in place, such as a lock on the case folder. No portable file-system lock stops a rename or a removal, and the case's own lock files are already that kind of lock. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D192), which R43 and P15 say since revision 22. It sees every replacement made before a file's last look, whatever the order, and leaves only the window after the last look, which no check can close. What (i) costs: three status calls for an ordinary reader's check and six for a holder's. The link-count test sees nothing the second pass of path looks misses, and R43 keeps it because D192 asks for it.

**Sub-decision: a key given more than once (revision 22, after verify-97 to verify-103; D192, in R4 since revision 22).** YAML lets a mapping give a key twice, and the standard loader silently keeps the last value, so a second category: inside a rule would win without anyone seeing it. (i) Refuse the load at the parser, naming the first repeat: what T01 built (D38), and what verify-97 failed, because that refusal hid every other fault in the table. (ii) Name the repeat as a fault, keep neither value, and still check every value given as a key given once is checked, whichever comes first: the rules of every rules list, and each value of a repeated field (d3d9f41, 0028830, 792727b and 4c017d4). (iii) Keep the last value, as the standard loader does: a table edit could then change a rule without anyone seeing it. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer, which R4 says since revision 22 (D192). The table is refused either way, since the repeat is a fault, so checking each value changes only how many faults one refusal names. R4 has asked since revision 19 that a refusal name the table's faults together, never one only once another is mended. What (ii) costs: each value of a repeated field is checked once, alone, with the rule's other repeated fields left unbuilt, so a check that reads two fields, such as the bank's code, grants no exemption on a guess. At 4c017d4 only the last value is compiled and built, and the pattern-form checks read none of the values (Context); T03 extends them before its review.

**Sub-decision: how far R4's naming reaches in a table that is not well formed (revision 23; D210).** Since revision 19 R4 has asked a refusal to name every fault of a table beside the others: a malformed value whatever else its rule carries, a pattern unusable in a form the matcher builds, and since revision 22 every value of a key given more than once. After revision 22, eight reviews (verify-105 to verify-119) each found one more fault that a repeated key, a value YAML cannot build or a pattern that does not compile kept the refusal from naming, and each of those tables was refused all the same. (i) Name every fault and every combination of a repeated key's values, as revisions 19 to 22 asked. (ii) Refuse a table that is not well formed as a whole, naming at least one fault its loader finds, and keep naming by rule for a well-formed table. (iii) Refuse every table at its first fault, naming only that one. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D210), which R4 and R54 say since revision 23. (i) lost because each review found one more way a file can be wrong, while the guarantee the underwriter needs, that no such table loads or categorises a line, held throughout. (iii) lost because a well-formed table's exceptions are what R4 exists to refuse: a table edit that hides one list's wording behind an unless, a lookaround or an anchor on two rules should be refused naming both, and that check has been stable since D126. What (ii) costs: a person mending a hand-edited table with several structural faults may see them one at a time; nothing loads meanwhile, and reinstalling the package restores the shipped tables. 'Not well formed' is read to take in a value of a kind its field does not allow and a pattern unusable in a form the matcher builds, and 'the faults the loader finds' as at least one (Context). T03 changes nothing: 3ae10cc names more than R4 now asks, and its repeated-key handling can be made simpler after T03 passes.

**Sub-decision: lock-file interference and interrupted saves (revision 23; D210).** Since revision 10 R42 has put removing or replacing a case lock file outside its single-writer guarantee, and since revision 17 R43 has put interference beyond a read across a lock file the reader counts on outside what a reader can know (D99, D144). Revision 13 added two guarantees that hold under such interference: two saves told recorded at one log position leave a log reported broken, never intact, and a log that reads intact holds every save told recorded (D124). Revisions 14 to 22 built the read-back, the take-back and the reader's rereads that keep them, and the reviews since verify-64 kept finding one more interleaving, latterly with an interruption: Ctrl-C, a signal handler or a callback that runs inside a save. (i) Put every guarantee under lock-file interference or an interruption at an arbitrary instant outside R42, R43 and P15: the tool keeps the clauses as far as it can, reports as it can, and a check after the interference stops gives the true answer (design default). (ii) Put the reader's reports and an interrupted save's report outside, and keep revision 13's two guarantees under lock-file interference. (iii) As revision 22. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D211), as the reading of D210, which says the tool reports 'as it can'. (iii) lost on D210. (ii) loses because the read-back and the take-back are how R42 keeps those two guarantees, and verify-112 to verify-118 each found a read-back across a replaced lock file that went one step wrong; under (ii) the next review would look for the next one. What (i) costs: under lock-file interference or an interruption, a save told recorded can be missing from a log that reads intact, or a save can get a report that another answer would fit better, until a check after the interference or a comparison with a signed pack shows it. A hand edit of the case folder could always do as much (D103), and the interference needs someone or some tool removing the tool's own files, or an interrupt landing at a particular instant. The protection left is the hash chain, the sign-off's whole-log check while holding the case, the head a signed pack pins, and every guarantee as written for cases with no such interference. *The nested save:* (a) R42 keeps its refusal of a save begun inside another on the same thread, before it writes anything, and D210's 'a nested save from a handler' is read as a way of interrupting the save it runs inside, so what is outside is what that save then does, reports or leaves behind (design default); (b) the nested save's refusal goes outside too. (a) is what 57984ea makes one step and 46b03be registers inside its cleanup region, and no review since verify-106 has found a fault in it; without it the growth check would refuse the outer save instead, losing the save the person started. The owner chose (a) (D212).

**Sub-decision: what P2 asks of each kind of figure (revision 24, after verify-127 and verify-128; D219).** P2 said that the signed amounts of a sum figure's lines total it, and named no sums. Read as every GBP figure, it failed M8, M11, M12 and M14, which report money paid out as positive amounts, and M9, which counts a returned transfer only up to what was sent. (i) Make every GBP figure a sum: report M8, M11, M12 and M14 as negative amounts, and cite for M9 only the lines that count in full. That changes four of M1-M16's values, which R10 forbids; the packaged policy compares M8 and M11 as positive amounts, and signed memos cite them so. A line is in M9's evidence wholly or not at all, so a capped credit would leave M9's evidence, hiding the line a person should look at. (ii) Three kinds. A sum figure's lines total it. An outflow figure is reported as a positive amount and its lines total it negated. A derived figure cites every line its calculation reads and is not asked to total them, and M9 names in an evidence note the lines it counts only in part. (iii) Keep P2's words and let each build say which figures are sums: each review would read 'sum' afresh, as verify-127 and verify-128 did. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D219), which P2, R1, R11 and R16 say since revision 24. What (ii) costs: a person checking an outflow by hand negates its lines' total, which the drill-down shows and names (R16), and M9 cannot be checked by adding its lines; its method and its note say what counts. *The monthly money-out cell:* (a) an outflow figure, a positive amount whose lines total it negated, like M8 (design default); (b) a signed sum, a negative amount its lines total, as D219's 'every monthly cell is a sum figure' reads on its own. (a) because a monthly table shows money out beside money in as a positive amount, as a bank statement's paid-out column does, and D219 makes every figure shown as money paid out an outflow figure. (b) would have every flow cell total its lines with no sign rule, at the cost of a money-out column that is negative while M8 to M14 are positive. Either way the balance and overdrawn-day cells are derived. *Chose (a)*, the owner's answer (D220), which R11 has said since revision 24 and cites since revision 25; T06, which builds the monthly summary, has not started.

**Sub-decision: how every figure stays to the penny (revision 25, after verify-129; D223, in R2 since revision 25).** The figures layer counts in integer pence (R2), but Transaction and Metric carry money as floats, which the evidence record, MCP, the checkpoint allow-list and the policy read. verify-129 found three ways a float lost or hid a penny: the loader read the text as a float, lines a float each carried added up to a total it did not, and two-place formatting rounded the binary value. (i) Read the text exactly, and refuse at import a statement with an amount or balance, or a money in or money out, of £10,000,000,000,000 or more, below which fifteen significant digits carry every amount and figure exactly (0c63ca4). (ii) Carry money as integer pence or decimals in the models: the float goes, but Transaction.amount and Metric.value change type for every reader, the evidence JSON among them, whose fields R53 keeps, and a checkpoint written before would not resume as the same type. (iii) Keep the float and withhold, as n/a with the reason, every figure no float carries, as the build before 0c63ca4 did for M17: verify-129 failed that, since a statement whose lines are all valid then gets no total money in at all. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D223), which R2 says since revision 25. A small business's statement is many orders of magnitude below the limit, so the refusal meets no real file, and the check is made once, where a statement enters, instead of at every figure. What (i) costs: one check in the loader and in each importer, and a statement at the limit is refused rather than reported. The figures layer keeps its round-trip check as a defence for a Statement built without the loader, and M3, which can exceed M2 over a period shorter than a month, stays n/a with the reason when no float carries it. *A case of more than one account:* R41 combines the accounts' flows, so two statements each below the limit could give a combined money in that no float carries to the penny. (a) Refuse the import that would bring a case's combined money in or money out to the limit, naming the account (design default; C3, C4). (b) Show the combined flow figures as n/a with the reason, as R41 does for balances over dates that do not overlap. (c) Nothing, which leaves R2's 'every money figure to the penny' unmet for such a case. (a) because it keeps one rule, refused where money enters, for a statement and for a case. *Chose (a)*, the owner's answer (D224), which R41 says since revision 26. R41 counts each account's flows in full, before own-account pairs are removed, so the check is made when the account is imported; multi-account cases are Tier 2, so no task built yet is affected.

**Sub-decision: which lines a derived figure cites (revision 25, after verify-129; D223, in P2, R10 and R54 since revision 25).** Revision 24 gave a derived figure 'every line its calculation reads' and read that, for a lowest balance, as the line that set it. verify-129 read it for days overdrawn (M6) as every close the count reads, in credit or not. (i) M1 to M16 keep the evidence the previous release gave them; the new derived figures and the monthly balance and overdrawn-day cells cite every line their calculation reads. (ii) Every derived figure cites every line its calculation reads, M5 and M6 included: M6 would cite every close in the period and M5 every line, which changes evidence the dual run compares and signed memos cite, and makes an overdrawn day harder, not easier, to find. (iii) Every derived figure cites the lines that decide it, as M5 and M6 do: a reviewer would read 'the lines that decide it' afresh for each new figure, as verify-129 read 'every line its calculation reads'. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D223), which P2, R10 and R54 say since revision 25. What (i) costs: two rules for one kind of figure, which P2 names figure by figure, and a month's days-overdrawn cell cites more lines than M6 cites for the same days. *The month's lowest end-of-day balance:* (a) every close in the month, as D223's words read and as the month's average and days-overdrawn cells cite (design default); (b) the line that set it, as M5 cites its lowest balance. (a) because a reviewer reads 'every line its calculation reads' at its finest grain, as verify-129 did, and a month has at most thirty-one closes, which the drill-down lists beside the inputs and the method (R16). (b) is the line a person checking the lowest balance looks at first, and P2 would then name that cell's evidence apart from the rule. *Chose (a)*, the owner's answer (D225), which P2 and R11 have said since revision 25; T06, which builds the monthly summary, has not started.

**Sub-decision: where money is quoted to the penny (revision 26, after verify-131; D229, in R2 since revision 26).** R2 said that every money figure is reported to the penny. verify-131 read that as reaching the memo's prose, which quotes a £0.60 payout as £1, as the previous release quoted every money figure. (i) To the penny everywhere, prose included: the prompt's metrics table and the offline model's prose would change, the committed example's prose would read differently from every memo the previous release wrote, and the guard would hold a hosted model to pence it has no reason to quote. (ii) To the penny where figures are recorded or tabulated, the evidence record, the appendix's figure table, the interactive table and every table built later; prose, and the prompt table and rule sentences it is drafted from, keep the whole pound, which the guard checks at the precision written. (iii) Prose to the penny for the money-in buckets only, so that the buckets quoted add up to M17 quoted: two conventions in one paragraph. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D229), which R2 says since revision 26. A credit memo quotes money in pounds, a person checking a figure reads the appendix or the drill-down, where the pence are, and the prose stays what the previous release wrote. What (ii) costs: the prose can quote buckets whose whole pounds do not add up to M17's whole pound, as rounded figures can; the appendix beside it shows the pence that do. *The pack's memo-ready section text* (R26): (a) the whole pound, as the memo's prose (design default, as R2 says since revision 26); (b) to the penny, as the appendix's figure table. (a) because the sections are the memo the underwriter copies, the guard checks them as it checks the memo, and the record beside them carries every figure to the penny with its lines. (b) suits a firm whose template quotes pence, at the cost of sections that read differently from the memo `cma run` drafts. *Chose (a)*, the owner's answer (D230), which R2 has said since revision 26; the firm's template is still open (E8), and T24, which builds the sections, has not started.

**Sub-decision: M1 to M16 with a case profile (revision 26, after verify-130 and verify-131; D229, in R54, P2, P7 and R10 since revision 26).** M16 and M26 share one denominator, money in less lending drawdowns, returned direct debits and own-account transfers (Data changes). The previous release had no own-account category, so under v1 on its own no line is one, and M16 reads the previous release's lines. A case profile can name an own account, and under v1 a credit naming it is then an own-account transfer (src/cma/categorise.py:1302-1304 at 74ad8af), which leaves M16's denominator and evidence. (i) A case profile is new behaviour under any ruleset: M16 and M26 leave its own-account transfers out of their share, and M1 to M16 keep the previous release's lines only for a statement categorised by v1 on its own with no case profile. (ii) Under v1 a case profile is ignored, so v1 is the previous release exactly, with or without a case: the rollback flag would then drop every director and own account the underwriter entered, and a case rolled back to v1 would read differently from the same case under v2 for a reason no one sees. (iii) Under v1, M16 keeps own-account transfers in its denominator while M26 leaves them out: two denominators for two shares of one money in, which the design has kept as one since M26 was added. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D229). What (i) costs: under v1 with a profile, M16's lines and value can differ from what the previous release would give the same statement; no committed or synthetic case carries a profile, so no committed figure moves. R5 and R6 apply under v1 as under v2, so a credit both kinds claim is ambiguous there too (C1).

**Sub-decision: M1 to M16 where the previous release's float sums were not exact (revision 27, after verify-133; D234, in R2, R10, R54, P2, P7 and the term derived figure since revision 27).** The previous release adds amounts as binary floats and rounds each result to the penny (src/cma/metrics.py:161-162 at e695f55). On Python 3.11, whose sum adds floats one at a time, a large enough statement can drift half a penny, and the previous release then reports a penny that no line holds: on verify-133's statement, every loan repayment returned unpaid, it reports £0.01 of debt service (M8), and debt service cover (M10) cites every operating, drawings and loan line. Python 3.12 and later sum with compensation and give the exact pence there, so on that statement the previous release gives two answers, one per interpreter. (i) R2's exact pence prevail; each figure's evidence follows them by the previous release's rule, R54 promises the previous release's lines only where its float sums came to the exact pence, and the dual run compares the statements on which the two agree (D234). (ii) Reproduce the previous release's float sums for the decisions that choose evidence, so M10 cites what the previous release cited: the figures layer would do money arithmetic in floats, which R2 forbids, the answer would change with the Python version, and on 3.11 M10 would rest on a penny of debt service that no line holds, the invented number the bet exists to stop. (iii) Lower the import limit until no float sum can drift half a penny: the drift grows with the number of lines as well as the amounts, so no bound on amounts closes it, and the limit would refuse statements R2 has accepted since revision 25 (D223). *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D234). What (i) costs: on such a statement, a rollback to v1 on Python 3.11 does not reproduce the previous release's M8 and M10, which differ only by the penny the floats made; no committed fixture, synthetic case or small business's statement comes near it. *How the dual run compares only statements where both agree:* its tests compare M1 to M16's values before their evidence (tests/test_previous_release.py:129-130 and :149-150), and run the previous release in a child of the same interpreter (tests/previous_release.py:33), so a statement whose float sums drift fails on the value and is never compared on evidence alone. Its statements are the synthetic archetypes and the committed example, far below any drift, and CI runs them on Python 3.11, 3.12 and 3.13 (.github/workflows/ci.yml:17), so no dual-run test changes. verify-133's statement belongs instead to a test of R2 and P2 that needs no previous release: M8 £0.00 and M10 n/a with no lines, on every supported Python.

**Sub-decision: a resumed review whose policy outcome the changed figures leave open (revision 28, after verify-144 to verify-146; D249, in R53 since revision 28).** A review that an earlier release left pending holds that release's figures, which can be a penny from the exact pence and lack M17 and the money-in buckets, and a policy result computed from them. Recomputed here, a figure a policy rule reads can move. Where it moves within the rule's band, the stored rule's comparison and limits give its outcome. Where it newly fails or becomes n/a, the outcome needs the rule's fail or when-missing action, which only the policy holds. (i) Resume on the stored figures and the stored result, as before verify-142: verify-141 and verify-142 failed it, since the figures shown and recorded would not be this release's (R1, R2). (ii) Recompute the figures and take each changed rule's outcome from the stored result where it settles it. Otherwise use the policy file the review was drafted with, only if its hash is the stored one. Otherwise refuse, naming the figure and saying to run `cma run` again, with the review left pending and nothing recorded (D249; 94ced9c). (iii) Store a copy of the policy in every new checkpoint, so that a later release can always settle a rule: it does nothing for reviews already pending, which hold no copy, and it adds a field the checkpoint allow-list and every resume carry. (iv) Evaluate with whatever policy file is at the stored path, or the packaged default: the outcome would come from a policy the review was not drafted with, and a file swapped while the review waits would change what is recorded. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D249), which R53 states since revision 28. (iii) is outside this release, as D249 says. What (ii) costs: a review cannot be resumed if the earlier release left it pending, its figure newly fails a rule or becomes n/a, and its policy file has moved, changed or was never hashed. The reviewer then drafts again with `cma run`. Once this release rewords R9's description in the packaged policy (Migration), the packaged file's hash changes, so for a review drafted with the packaged policy only the stored result can settle a rule. Each case needs a review left pending across the upgrade and a figure that moves across a rule's edge, as near the import limit or at a rounding midpoint.

**Sub-decision: what a Monzo refusal says, and where its reading stops (revision 29, after verify-149 to verify-153; D258, in R2 since revision 29).** The Monzo importer converts an export row by row, and each review found one more field whose problem, on a later row or on the row that takes money in or out to the limit, was reported without saying that the rows already read had reached the limit. (i) Read every row and every field and name every problem, as the loader does for a five-column statement: each review asked for one more combination, and an export with a record the CSV reader cannot read cannot be read to its end. (ii) Read the rows in order and stop at the first problem, reading a row's currency and then, for a row that counts, its amount before its other fields, and say with that problem whether money in or money out is at the limit among the rows read so far (D258; c2460a0 but for the Transaction ID). (iii) Total every row's amount in a pass of its own before any other check: a row whose amount cannot be read cannot be totalled, and the pass would read rows the importer goes on to refuse. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D258). What (ii) costs: an export whose first problem comes before the rows that take money in to the limit is refused naming that problem alone; once it is mended, the next import says the limit. A row in another currency is still read as far as its currency and its Transaction ID, as the previous release read it, so an export that imported before still imports and one refused before is still refused (R53).

**Sub-decision: how a refusal names a line, and what it says of a field too long to read (revision 29, after verify-147 and verify-148; D258, in R2 since revision 29).** (i) The CSV reader's own count, which names a record by its last line: a refusal could name a description's continuation (verify-147). (ii) The line of the file on which the record starts, the header and blank lines counted, as a person sees the file in an editor (9efc40c). (iii) The record's number among the records: no editor shows it. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D258). A field longer than the CSV reader reads, 131,072 characters by default, stops the reader. (a) Say that a field is too long to read, naming the line its record starts on, and nothing about the limit (D258). (b) Say that such a field would be past the limit if it were an amount or balance, as c2460a0's CLI text does: false for a field of leading zeros, and the field may be a description. *Chose (a)*. It costs nothing: the statement is refused either way, and the records before it are checked.

**Sub-decision: how processes that share a checkpoint file take turns (revision 29, after verify-149 to verify-153; D258, in R53 since revision 29).** `cma run` and `cma resume` can share one SQLite checkpoint file across processes, and each review found one more interleaving: a refresh written into a review decided meanwhile, a comparison and its write that were two operations, two runs starting one review id. (i) A lock file beside the checkpoint file, held while a run starts a review, while a resume compares a pending review and refreshes it, and while a decision is applied (6b2f80c, c2460a0). (ii) SQLite's own write transaction around each step: the LangGraph saver opens and commits its own transactions, and starting a review spans several checkpoints and a model call. (iii) No lock, and a comparison of the checkpoint before each write: verify-152 showed that the comparison and the write are two operations. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D258). It is D99's answer for a case's files: the lock orders the tool's own processes, and another program writing to the checkpoint file, removing or replacing the lock file, or a process stopped part-way through a write is outside the guarantee. What (i) costs: a run holds the lock while it drafts, a hosted model call included, so another process on the same checkpoint file waits for it; the lock file stays beside the checkpoint file and holds no data; on Windows the lock call gives up after about ten seconds until T04 makes it wait (Context).

**Sub-decision: how the memo and its evidence are written (revision 29, after verify-152 and verify-153; D258, in R53 since revision 29).** The export is of the decided review, so the decision is recorded in the checkpoint before either file is written. (i) Make both in full, write each beside its target and move it into place, the evidence first (c2460a0): a failure making them writes nothing, and no memo is in place without its evidence. (ii) Write the files before recording the decision: they would carry a review not yet decided, and a decision refused as made after another would leave them behind. (iii) Record in the checkpoint whether the files were written, so that a second `cma resume` could write a decided review out again: a new state field and command, outside this release. *Chose (i)*, the owner's answer (D258). A disk failure, a full disk or an interruption between the decision and both files being in place is outside the guarantee; the decision stays recorded, and a second export is outside this release. *An output path taken while the review waits:* the paths are checked as free before the review is shown, and the reviewer may take minutes. (a) Outside the guarantee, like another program writing to the checkpoint file (design default, as R53 reads D258 since revision 29): a file another program or a second `cma run` with the same --out puts there meanwhile is replaced, and a folder there makes the move fail after the decision is recorded. (b) Move each file into place only where no file is, with a hard link, as the first empty head record is created since revision 15, so that a taken path is reported and nothing at it is replaced; the decision stays recorded, as after any failure. (a) costs no build change, and lets a second run to the same --out replace the first run's memo and evidence. (b) costs one change in T04 and a file system with hard links, which the case store already needs. *Chose (a)*, the owner's answer (D259).

**Sub-decision: how much of a file is read before its first record is checked (revision 30, after verify-178 to verify-185; D289, in R2 since revision 30).** (i) Read the file as a stream: decode it as it is read and feed the CSV reader line by line from the file, so that a refusal reads nothing after the record it names but the reader's look at the next. A byte-order mark, a file whose lines end in CR alone, the decoder's and the CSV reader's own buffering and the test for a quoted field open to the end of the file would each need code of its own, and each review would test one more. (ii) Read the file into memory, decode it and split it into lines, then read its records in one pass, one record ahead (82758a3). (iii) As (ii), but check each record before the next is read: a record whose last field is a quoted field left open to the end of the file cannot then be told from a last record, since only the next read finds the end. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D289), which R2 says since revision 30. The previous release read every record into memory before checking any (src/cma/ingest.py:63-67 and src/cma/importers/monzo.py:123-132 on the integration branch), so (ii) reads no more than it did. What (ii) costs: a refusal of a file whose later part is very large comes only once that part has been read, decoded and split, and a file too large for memory is outside this release; a small business's statement export is far below either.

**Sub-decision: which checks the one-record-ahead limit holds (revision 31, after verify-188 to verify-192; D301, in R2 since revision 31).** Revision 30 said that the loader and the Monzo importer check each record once the next has been read. Read for every check that touches a record, that reaches checks that relate records to one another, and each review found one more of them made later. (i) Hold every check to one record ahead, as each review read revision 30. Which way a file runs is known from its dates, and the loader decides it from its first and last (src/cma/ingest.py:576-579 at bedf5ff), so a newest-first file's order and balance problems can be told from an oldest-first file's only once the file is read. A balance across a record with none can be checked only at the next record that has one. The Monzo importer walks the balance in date and time order, which it knows only once every row is read. Holding these to one record ahead would mean deciding a file's direction from its first two dates, which a file whose first lines share a date cannot give, and walking a Monzo export in file order, which changes which exports import. (ii) Hold a record's own values to one record ahead, and make the checks across records from the records read once reading has stopped. No order is promised between such a check and a record's own problem beyond what R2 says the Monzo importer names with its first problem (D301; bedf5ff). (iii) Make each check across records as early as it can be made, and state for each how long it may wait: one more rule per check for a review to test. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D301), which R2 says since revision 31. The previous release read every record before it checked any, so neither reader checks later than it did. What (ii) costs: the loader can name a balance that does not follow, or a date out of order, several records after the record it concerns. And the Monzo importer, stopping at a later row's own problem, names that problem and not a walked balance that already disagreed on an earlier row; the next import names that balance once the later row is mended.

**Sub-decision: the MCP server's ASGI apps (revision 30, after verify-122, verify-182 and verify-184; D289, in R29, R53 and P18 since revision 30).** The SDK's server gives an embedder two factories, sse_app() and streamable_http_app(), whose apps the embedder's own ASGI server binds and serves. (i) Hand out no app at all, so nothing the package hands out can listen under the lock (478e616): verify-182 failed it, since every embedder that mounts the app stops working with the lock off as well as on, which R53 rules out. (ii) While the lock is off, hand the app out behind a check that reads the lock at start-up and before every request and, once the lock is on, fails start-up, answers 503 and closes websockets; under the lock refuse; and state that a socket an embedder's server opens for a saved app before the app starts up is that server's doing (c1bbe1a, 6b43212). (iii) Hand the app out and read the lock again when the server binds: ASGI gives an app no such moment, and a server that binds before start-up, or is handed a bound socket, never calls the app first. *Chose (ii)*, the owner's answer (D289). What (ii) costs: an embedder that saved an app while the lock was off and starts it under the lock in a server with lifespan events turned off opens a listening socket. Every request on it gets 503 and no tool runs, so nothing is read or sent, but the port stays open until the embedder stops it. A server that starts the app up before it binds, as uvicorn does with lifespan events on, stops without binding. *What the app handed out is:* (a) the SDK's app behind the lock check, a wrapper whose every attribute but its call is the SDK app's, as 6b43212 builds; code that checks the app's class sees another class (design default; R53 since revision 30). (b) The SDK's own app, its class unchanged, with the lock check added to it as middleware that sees its start-up and every request, before it is handed out. (a) costs no build change. (b) costs one change in T17 and its two tests that assert the wrapper's class (tests/test_firm.py:600 and :663), and keeps an embedder's class check working. verify-184 named the class change as a minor. Revision 30 asked the owner, and D290 chose (a).

**Sub-decision: when a lock turned on takes effect for a run already going (revision 30, after verify-182 and verify-184; asked).** R29 is a state requirement: WHILE the lock is on, no hosted client is made and no network connection opened. verify-182 and verify-184 each probed a reading of firm.yaml used after the lock changed. 6b43212 binds a reading to its run and firm folder (Context), so the reading a run takes as it starts decides its hosted client, its hosted requests while it runs, and its tracing. (a) A lock turned on takes effect from the next run. A run keeps the lock its reading found, and its evidence records that lock truly. A resume reads firm.yaml as it resumes, and is under the lock if its draft was or if firm.yaml says so then. R29 and P18 say so in the next revision (design default; T17 makes the resume read firm.yaml rather than the graph's latest reading, src/cma/graph.py:427, before its review). (b) At once: every hosted request reads firm.yaml as it is made, so a run stops at its next request once the lock is on and writes no memo; tracing is still chosen as each invocation starts. (a) because a run is short and makes one draft, its evidence never records a lock other than the one that governed it, and a firm that turns the lock on mid-run gets the next run's refusal; it costs one line in T17. (b) closes the moment between a run's start and its last request, at the cost of a file read per request and a run that can fail part-way with nothing recorded. Revision 30 asked the owner, and D291 chose (a). R29 and P18 do not yet say it (next sub-decision).

**Sub-decision: when R29 and P18 say D291 (revision 31; asked).** D291 asked for its answer in R29 and P18 in the next revision. D301 keeps everything else in revision 30, so revision 31 does not write it. T17's reviews read R29, a state requirement, at its finest grain: verify-186, verify-189 and verify-191 each failed T17 on a reading of firm.yaml taken before the lock was turned on that still allowed a hosted client, a hosted request or tracing after it. (a) Write it into R29 and P18 in the next revision, with the answers to the next three questions, keeping every id (design default). (b) Leave R29 and P18 as revision 30 wrote them, so T17 is judged on a state requirement that says nothing of a run already going. (a) because D291 asked for it, and a requirement is judged by its own text (revision 8's lesson). Revision 31 asks the owner.

**Sub-decision: the window before a native MCP runner's bind (revision 31, after verify-191 and verify-193; asked).** R29 says that starting the MCP server through main() or its own run methods reads the lock first and is refused under it. A native HTTP runner reads the lock and then has Uvicorn bind its socket. With the lock turned on between the two, it binds under the lock (verify-193; src/cma/mcp_server.py:111 at 25db103). Since 25db103 the native runners serve the lock-checked app, so a request on such a socket gets 503 and no tool runs. (a) State the window, as R42 states the window between a writer's check and its step: a lock turned on after the runner's check and before its bind is outside R29 and P18, the socket serves no request, and the next start is refused (design default; no build change). (b) Read the lock again once the socket is bound, and close the socket if the lock is on. This narrows the window, leaves one between that read and the bind, and costs one change in T17. (c) Bind first, then read the lock and serve: under a lock already on, that opens a socket, which R29 rules out. (a) because no check can be joined to the bind, and because D291 already lets a lock turned on during a run take effect from the next one. Revision 31 asks the owner.

**Sub-decision: a callback a caller hands a run (revision 31, after verify-193; asked).** Under the lock the graph turns LangSmith's own tracing off (verify-182). verify-193 handed a run a LangChainTracer in its callbacks, and the tracer could export the run's data under the lock (src/cma/graph.py:535 at 25db103). 9b10daa, not yet reviewed, takes a LangSmith tracer out of a run's callbacks under the lock. (a) Take LangSmith tracers out under the lock, as 9b10daa does. Any other callback a caller hands a run is the caller's code, outside R29 as an embedder's own server is, and R29 says so in the next revision (design default).
