(b) Refuse, under the lock, a run handed any LangSmith tracer, naming the lock: the caller learns its tracer was not used, at the cost of a run that stops and one change in T17. (c) Take out every callback a caller hands a run under the lock: R29 would then cover code the tool did not write, and an embedder would lose its own progress callbacks. Revision 31 asks the owner.

**Sub-decision: what P5 counts as a sentinel (revision 31, after verify-186 to verify-193; asked).** P5 says that no prompt contains a sentinel string that the case's descriptions and profile contain, and R30 lets a prompt carry the rule table. Under a custom policy the rule table carries the policy's own rule ids, which the firm writes. T17 keeps a custom rule's id only when the rule is the packaged one and replaces it otherwise. Four reviews in a row failed that comparison: by id spelling; then without the referral bands; then without on_fail and when_missing; and, in verify-193, without the policy's provenance, where an exact copy of the packaged R2 under another policy name, with R2 used as a sentinel, put R2 in the prompt (src/cma/graph.py:196 at 25db103). A sentinel equal to a packaged rule id appears in every prompt drawn up with the packaged policy, whatever the build does, so P5 cannot hold for it as written. (a) A sentinel is a string of the case's own data that the policy in use does not hold. A string the packaged or a supplied policy holds is the firm's text, which R30 lets the rule table carry, and is outside P5; P5 and R30 say so in the next revision (design default; T17's comparison can then be simpler). (b) Keep P5 as written, and carry no supplied policy's rule ids or descriptions in a prompt: each custom rule shows as a position with its metric, comparison and limits, and the packaged rules keep their ids. A sentinel equal to a packaged id still needs (a)'s exception; the memo cites custom rules by position, and the guard and the appendix map them back. (a) because the policy is the firm's configuration, not applicant data, and the case cannot be kept from holding a string the firm also wrote. Revision 31 asks the owner.

## Decision

Build Option A as components inside the existing package. ADR 001 still holds: money is added up only in the figures layer, and the model never sees a statement.

    importers (csv, monzo, openbanking, truelayer, bank-csv)
          |
          v
    case store: inputs, profile, override log, case log
          |
          v
    loader and validation -> categoriser (rule tables, verdict, profile, firm rules, overrides)
          |
          v
    figures layer: M1-M28, money-in buckets, monthly summary, indicators, judgement queue (in pence)
          |
          +--> policy check (packaged illustrative policy; policy.evaluate, unchanged)
          |
          +--> review workspace (loopback; one process; renders only)
          |
          +--> preview (field map, section text, guard) --> sign-off (case log event, holding the case)
                    --> pack written once: memo.md, record.html, evidence.json, monthly.csv

**Components**
- **C1 Rule tables and categoriser** (src/cma/categorise.py; rule tables as data under src/cma/rules/). Built-in tables v1 (today's, frozen, first match wins) and v2 (the new lists, in two stages). v2 names cash paid in (counter, deposit machine, Post Office and PayPoint wording) as cash_deposit, and names cash-withdrawal wording that only the cash round-trip indicator reads. The ambiguity policy (R4) applies to credits under v2. Debits keep first-match order through the same stages: a debit that two rules match takes the first in table order, so the rule-order shuffle (Rollout) compares credits only. v1 is untouched (R54). Each line records which rule set its category.
  - *Stage 1, line-type cues*: explicit wording of what the line is. A returned payment, a move to or from the applicant's own pot, space or savings, HMRC, a refund or reversal; and two payment-method cues, which say how the money came when it did not come as cash: the payment rail (Faster Payment, BACS, CHAPS, a bank giro credit, a transfer, a standing order, a direct credit) and, since D70, a cheque. A payment-method cue has no bucket of its own. R4 names both since revision 7 (D76).
  - *Stage 2, name lists*: who sent the money or what it is for. Cash paid-in wording; lenders and merchant cash advance providers; director, capital-introduced and shareholder wording; generic loan and advance wording (loan, finance, cash advance, merchant advance, business advance; D71); acquirers, gateways, marketplaces and delivery platforms; invoice references; explained non-trading words (interest, grant, cashback, compensation, insurance claim).
  - *Whole words* (revision 4, sharpened in revision 5 by D64): a listed word or phrase, and a name from the case profile, matches only where the character on each side is not a letter, a digit or a combining mark in any script; a combining mark belongs to the word it follows. CAFÉSTRIPE LTD does not contain STRIPE, whether the É is one character or an E followed by a combining accent, and neither does किSTRIPE, whose vowel sign joins the word. Descriptions, listed names and profile names are compared in Unicode's composed form, upper case. Rules and profile names share this one definition.
  - *Separators* (revision 6, D71, D72): between any two characters of a listed name or a profile name, any run of separators may stand, or none: whitespace, dot, slash, the minus sign, every Unicode dash and every invisible format character (a soft hyphen, a zero-width space or joiner, a byte order mark). SUMUP, SUM UP, SUM-UP and SUM.UP are one name; FASTER–PAYMENT with an en dash, or with a zero-width space between its words, is FASTER PAYMENT. Every other character, an ampersand or a digit, matches only as listed. A listed name or profile value made only of separators names no one and matches nothing.
  - *Every occurrence* (revision 5, D64): a listed phrase is found wherever it starts, including inside another match of the same rule. In FROM POT TRANSFER CASH DEPOSIT the own-pot rule matches both FROM POT and POT TRANSFER, and the longer POT TRANSFER takes in TRANSFER, so no payment rail is left over and the own-account cue decides.
  - *The verdict on a credit*, in this order:
    1. Collect every credit rule that matches, from both stages, at every position where its wording occurs, overlapping occurrences included. A rule's own pattern and its names are scanned apart (D97). At each start, the rule's match is the longest that any of them, or any alternative inside a pattern, can make there (revision 10, D102; R4 since revision 11), so neither list order nor the order of alternatives picks a shorter match. A match inside a longer match from another rule counts only as the longer one (STRIPE inside STRIPE CAPITAL, LOAN inside DIRECTOR LOAN, REFUND inside HMRC REFUND, TRANSFER inside POT TRANSFER), except the bank's code at step 2, and cash paid-in wording and a payment-method cue inside each other's phrase at step 3 (D127, D132; R4 since revisions 14 and 15). Only a match that itself counts sets another aside (D141, R4 since revision 16): a match set aside inside a longer phrase sets nothing aside, so with FPS CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE on the cash list and FPS CASH DEPOSIT a gateway's name, the rail inside the gateway's phrase still counts (verify-71). It is settled match by match, from the longest down (D142; R4 since revision 17). Each match's standing rests only on longer matches, so there is one answer; a match set aside sets nothing aside even where its rule counts by another match; and a rule counts, and is a candidate, where any match of its own counts. 5c9a5bd settled it rule by rule, which verify-73 failed with FPS CASH DEPOSIT STRIPE | STRIPE; 4b3b21c settles it by match. What remains are the credit's matches.
    2. A returned-payment code the bank prints at the start of the line settles the line type on its own, because it is the bank's code, not payer wording. The codes are listed in code, not in the table (UNPAID DD), and only the returned-payment rule may carry one, alone (D96, D97). Since revision 10 (D101), and in R4 since revision 11, a longer phrase on another rule that begins with the code does not take it in: UNPAID DD TO SAVINGS is a returned payment, whatever list holds the longer phrase.
    3. A payment-method cue (the rail or a cheque) together with cash paid-in wording makes the credit ambiguous, whatever other cue matches (BACS CREDIT HMRC CASH PAID IN; CHQ CASH DEPOSIT): the line says both that the money came another way and that it came as cash. Cash wording listed as a cue meets the rail as cash wording on the name list does (D121). Cash wording counts here even inside a longer payment-method phrase on the table (FPS CASH DEPOSIT listed on the rail list; D127, R4 since revision 14; built in 1b592b6). Since revision 15 (D132), a payment-method cue counts here too inside a longer phrase on the cash list (FPS CASH DEPOSIT listed on the cash list), so the two never take each other in, whichever list holds the longer phrase; built in 78556a7.
    4. Otherwise, if any cue with a bucket matches, the cues decide: cues in one bucket give that bucket, and cues in two or more buckets make the credit ambiguous.
    5. With no cue that points to a bucket (R4's 'when no line-type cue points to a money-in bucket'), the names decide in the same way, whether or not a payment-method cue matched (FPS CREDIT STRIPE GRANT and CHQ STRIPE GRANT are ambiguous). A payment-method cue alone decides nothing. If no name matches either, nothing is decided, and the line falls through the precedence below to the profile, firm rules and the default.
    6. Inside the bucket decided, the category is the first in the table's declared category order that a deciding match carries: in trading receipts, cash deposit, then customer transfer, then card settlement; in refunds and returned payments, returned payment, then refund. Every other bucket has one category. The rule recorded is the first deciding match, in table order, that carries that category.
    7. An ambiguous credit's table candidates are all of its matches from step 1, from both stages, payment-method cues included, written in table order. Its category is other (unexplained). The profile's candidates join them at precedence step (4). A credit that the table does not find ambiguous but two kinds of profile name do (precedence step (4), revision 9) takes the same table candidates, all of its matches from step 1, not only the rule that would have decided it, and none when step 1 found nothing (D90).
  - *Bare words are not evidence* (D48): SAVINGS, SAVER, POT, SPACE or a pot label such as TAX POT without move wording, and CAPITAL outside capital introduced, injected or contributed, match no list.
  - *No hidden tie-breaks* (revision 6): a rule never carries an exception that hides another list's cue or name, and since D70 no v2 rule carries an exception at all: the cash rule's 'unless cheque' went when the cheque became a cue. Where two lists' wording meets, either a longer phrase goes on one list, or the credit is ambiguous. Since revision 9 the same holds between the profile's two kinds of name, and since revision 10 between the alternatives inside one pattern. verify-65 and verify-67 showed that a table edit could still add an exception; since D126 a v2 table that gives any rule one is refused at load, naming the rule (R4 since revision 14; 4adddf9). verify-69 wrote the same exception into a pattern as a lookahead; since D136 a v2 pattern with a lookahead or lookbehind is refused too (R4 since revision 15; 78556a7). An anchor does the same for the whole line; since D137 a v2 pattern with one is refused too, except the bank's code listed in code (R4 since revision 16; 2c72469).
  - *Table validation* (revision 6; revision 10, D94, D96, D97; revision 11, D110; revision 13, D115-D121; revision 14, D126; revision 15, D136; revision 16, D137; revision 21, D168; revision 22, D192): a table is refused at load, naming the rule, in nine cases. First, a pattern that does not compile in every form the matcher uses, including the form that finds matches at every position (a global inline flag such as (?i)INV[0-9]+ is one way to fail). Second, a v2 pattern that uses a back-reference or a conditional group, whose meaning would change when the matcher wraps it, or an atomic group or a possessive repeat, which no later end can reopen, so the longest match could not be found (D117). Third, a v2 pattern that can match no text, such as a lookahead alone, which leaves no span for the longer-phrase rule to set aside (D121). Fourth, since revision 11 (D110), a v2 pattern with a repeat whose body can match both a space and a letter or digit (.*, [A-Z ]+), whose match would reach across words and take in other lists' wording. Fifth, the bank's code: the bank-code flag anywhere but on the returned-payment rule whose whole pattern is one of the bank return codes listed in code, with no names, no exception and a direction that includes credits; and a v2 table in which a listed code has no rule that carries the flag, or carries the code's pattern without it (D96, D97, D115, D117, D119). Sixth, a v2 rule id that holds anything but letters, digits, _, . and -, or that is default, round_trip or conflict, the categoriser's own provenance (D119, D121); an id that is not a string is named like any other malformed field, never raised as an error (verify-67's and verify-71's minor and verify-77's major; R4 says so since revision 19, for every rule field; verify-79 found the last such path, the gathering of the codes the bank-code rules carry, for a bank-code rule whose pattern is a list, which 5139e22 closes at :534-535; since revision 20 R4 says a malformed value is named by its rule whatever else the rule carries, the bank-code flag included, D163). Seventh, since D126 (R4 since revision 14; 4adddf9), an exception, an unless or a requires, on any v2 rule, which could switch a rule off where another list's wording sits and so decide a collision before the ambiguity check sees it (verify-65, verify-67). Eighth, since D136 (R4 since revision 15; 78556a7), a v2 pattern with a lookahead or lookbehind, an exception written into the pattern, which tests wording beside its match without matching it (verify-69). The whole-word test the matcher adds is the matcher's own, and \b written in a pattern looks only at the character on each side, as whole words do, so neither is an exception. Ninth, since D137 (R4 since revision 16; 2c72469), a v2 pattern with an anchor (^, $, \A or \Z), which tests whether anything stands before or after its match in the line; only the bank's code listed in code keeps its ^, on the rule that carries the bank-code flag. So a table never loads and then fails on the first credit it matches, a table edit cannot give payer wording the bank's standing or switch the bank's code off, no pattern can hide another list's words by reaching over them, no rule can hide them behind an exception, in its keys or in its pattern, and a line's provenance always names one decision. Only the frozen v1 table is spared these checks (R4 since revision 18, D153, after verify-75; 9042fb4), and the seventh to ninth, the exception checks, only as its bytes were verified (R4 since revision 21, D168, after verify-81; 5f5bb2e). Only a digest of its bytes that matches the pin verifies them: a table checked with no digest, or with another, has its exceptions named whatever it says of itself (R4 since revision 22, D192, after verify-103; 4c017d4). The table the system loads as v1 is judged by its bytes before anything else in it is checked (R4 and R54 since revision 19, D158, after verify-77; 7cb2e71): with any bytes but the pinned ones it is refused as a changed frozen table, naming the table, whatever it says of itself, with whatever else keeps it from loading named after that and none of its rules used, whatever the change makes fail (R4 since revision 20, D163, after verify-79; 5139e22, which catches every failure of a changed v1, a type error included). A changed v1 names, after the changed-table refusal, each of its rules that carries an exception. Beyond that it is judged as the kind of table it says it is: a copy that still calls itself frozen is not held to the checks only the maintained lists need (stages, the category order, rule ids and the v2 pattern forms), which v1's own rules lack, and a copy set to frozen: false gets them all (R4 since revision 21). Every table not loaded as v1, whether or not it calls itself frozen, gets every check and is refused naming the rule. Every rule's matchers, in every form the matcher uses, are built while the table is checked, so a pattern that compiles alone but cannot be used is named with the table's other faults, whatever else its rule carries, never found once they are mended (R4 since revision 21, D168, after verify-81; 5f5bb2e; for a rule with another malformed field since 48c5b9c and edc3ff4, after verify-83 and verify-85). A key given more than once, in the table or in a rule, is a fault named with the others, and every value it was given is still checked as a key given once is. That means the rules of every rules list, and each value of a repeated field, compiled, built and given every check a pattern given once gets, whichever value comes first. R4 says so since revision 22 for compiling, building and exceptions (D192, after verify-97 to verify-103). At 4c017d4 only the last value is compiled and built, and the checks for a back-reference, no text, an atomic group or a possessive repeat and a repeat across words read none of the values (Context). A failure that no check foresees is still refused, but at 4c017d4 its refusal names no rule (src/cma/categorise.py:886-892). Since revision 23 (D210, after verify-105 to verify-119) R4 draws a line at a well-formed table: every key given once, every value one YAML can build and of the kind its field allows, every pattern compiling alone and in every form the matcher builds, and no field contradicting another. A table that is not well formed is refused as a whole, naming at least one fault its loader finds, and none of its rules is used; naming every fault, or every combination of a repeated key's values, is outside R4. A well-formed table gets every check above, each naming its rule. At 3ae10cc the validator names more than that, and load_ruleset turns any other failure into a refusal (src/cma/categorise.py:977-983), so T03 changes nothing for revision 23.
  - *Reversal linking* (revision 10, after verify-53): under v2 a returned payment is linked to the debit it undoes by amount and payee, the payee read with the returned-payment wording the table recognises set aside, so RETURNED DD IWOCA LTD links to DD IWOCA LTD as UNPAID DD IWOCA LTD does. v1 links as it always has (R54).
  - *Profile names* (revision 7, D78; revision 8, D81, D82; revision 9, D86, D88): R5 and R6 speak of a line whose counterparty matches a name. A bank line has no separate payer field, so a profile name, like a listed name, matches wherever its words occur in the description, under the same whole-word and separator rules. The description cannot tell the payer from a payment reference that names someone else. Each kind is matched on its own: a director's name inside an own account's name (JANE DOE in JANE DOE LTD) matches both, because step 1's longer-phrase rule settles the table's rules, not who is mentioned. So a profile name decides a line only below a protected verdict. It decides a credit only when the table finds the wording unambiguous and no other kind of profile name matches; otherwise it adds its bucket as one more candidate and settles nothing. A credit that both a director's and an own account's names match is ambiguous whatever the table finds, unless a protected verdict decides it (R5, R6, revision 9). Its candidates are every rule it matched in step 1, from both stages, then profile:director and profile:own_account. Under a protected verdict no name is read and none adds a candidate. With Jane Doe named as a director: REFUND JANE DOE is a refund, JANE DOE LOAN a lending drawdown, and FPS CREDIT DIRECTOR LOAN JANE DOE director funding with the table's rule, not the profile's. With ACME HOLDINGS an own account as well, IWOCA JANE DOE ACME HOLDINGS is a lending drawdown. On a debit, which is never ambiguous, the protected rules come first in table order and an own account's name after them (R6). R5 speaks only of credits, so a debit naming a director and an own account is an own-account transfer, and a repayment to a named lender that mentions an own account is debt service. Under v1 the profile is new behaviour too (R54 since revision 26, D229): a line takes v1's protected rules first and the profile's names after them, as the frozen path does (src/cma/categorise.py:1299-1304 at 74ad8af), and a credit both kinds claim is ambiguous as R5 and R6 say, with every v1 rule it matches and both profile kinds as its candidates. At 74ad8af the frozen path takes the director's name first; T05, which builds R5 and R6, makes it ambiguous.
  - *Precedence*, highest first:
    1. A per-line override.
    2. A protected verdict: the line-type cues plus lender, director, capital-introduced, shareholder and generic loan and advance wording (R21's patterns and the cues; the requirements' term since revision 8, which R5 and R6 name).
    3. An own-account pairing (R8), matched by amount and date across the case's accounts, not by text.
    4. An ambiguous verdict, settling nothing (R4, R5, R6; D78, D86). It applies when the table finds the wording ambiguous (R4) or, since revision 9, when both a director's or shareholder's and an own account's names match (R5, R6). It puts the credit in unexplained money in with every candidate recorded: its table candidates from step 7, then one candidate for each kind of case-profile name its wording matches, a director or shareholder (profile:director) before an own account (profile:own_account).
    5. A case-profile name (R5, R6) on a line the table does not find ambiguous, that no protected verdict settled and that only one kind of profile name matches; on a debit, an own account's name (R6).
    6. Firm rules, which therefore never reach a line settled at (2) to (5) (R21, R4).
    7. An unprotected verdict: the trading lists, invoice references, cash paid in and explained non-trading words.
    8. The default, where a debit is supplier and a credit is unexplained.
    Revision 7 moved the profile name from above the ambiguous verdict to below it and kept every other relative order of revision 6. Revision 8 changed no step, and R5 and R6 state step (2)'s place above (4) and (5). Revision 9 moves a credit that both kinds of profile name match from (5) to (4). A debit is never ambiguous, so a profile name on a debit (R6) is read at (5). Revisions 10 to 14 change no step.
  - Cash paid in is not protected (revision 2 never listed it), so a director named in the profile outranks cash wording, and a director's cash is director funding (R5). The exceptions: when the table finds the credit ambiguous (cash wording with a payment-method cue, or with a second bucket's name), or an own account's name also matches, the director's name is one more candidate. Every protected verdict, and every category a profile name or a pairing gives, is non-trading. So the only routes into trading receipts are an unambiguous trading verdict, a firm rule on a line that steps (2) to (5) did not settle, and a person's override.
  - *What C1 hands on* (revision 4; revision 7): each line's category and rule, an ambiguous credit's conflict form and candidates included, for any rule id of a table the category that rule sets (none for a payment-method cue), and for a profile candidate the category its kind sets (director_funding, own_account_transfer). C1 does not build the judgement queue; C2 assembles it and C5 shows it (R15).
- **C2 Figures layer** (src/cma/metrics.py, new src/cma/indicators.py). Adds M17-M28, the monthly summary and the indicators, including the three cash indicators. cash_deposit is a trading-receipts category, so M2 and every figure built on it include cash, and M18 reports the cash inside M2. All sums and threshold comparisons are in integer pence. The pence come from amounts the loader read exactly and held below £10,000,000,000,000, where the float the models carry reads back as the counted pence (R2 since revision 25); the figures layer still refuses a float that does not, for a Statement built without the loader. Each figure comes with its evidence and is one of three kinds (P2 since revision 24, D219). A sum figure (M2, M17, M18, M19 to M25, and the monthly money-in and trading-receipts cells) comes with the total of its lines' signed amounts, which equals it. An outflow figure (M8, M11, M12, M14 and the monthly money-out cell) is reported as a positive amount and comes with its lines' signed total, which is the figure negated. Every other figure is derived and comes with no total. A derived figure among M1 to M16 cites its evidence by the previous release's rule, M5 the line that set the lowest balance and M6 the line that closed each overdrawn day, and the previous release's lines for a statement categorised by v1 on its own with no case profile on which the previous release's float sums came to the exact pence, the previous release's rule applied to the exact pence on any other, so that M10 cites no lines when exact debt service is £0.00 (R10, R54; revision 27, D234); M16, like M26, leaves out of its denominator the own-account transfers a case profile or a pairing makes under any ruleset (R54 since revision 26, D229); every other derived figure cites every line its calculation reads, for one read from end-of-day balances the line that set the close of every day it reads (P2 since revision 25). M9 also comes with an evidence note naming, by id, the lines it counts only in part; the note sits beside its evidence, never in its method sentence, which the prompt and the appendix show (R30). A case whose buckets do not reconcile is refused. C2 also assembles the judgement queue as a pure function of the categorised lines, the pairing candidates and the indicators (R15): unexplained lines, ambiguous credits among them, possible own-account transfers and every flagged line, largest amount first. An ambiguous credit's entry carries its candidates, each with the category C1 says its rule or profile kind sets and the bucket that category maps to.
- **C3 Case store** (new src/cma/case.py). Case root, case folders, profile, override log, the hash-chained case log with its head record, its two lock files and the single-writer rule, snapshot hash, purge and purge ledger, and the git-ignore and sync-folder checks.
  - *Concurrency scope* (revision 5; R42, R43, P15): one writer at a time holds a case, whether the writers are windows, commands, threads or separate CaseLog objects in one process. A check is not a writer (revision 8, D80). A writer takes the case by passing the gate alone and briefly, then taking the case lock alone, and leaves the gate at once whether it took the case or was refused (revision 10, D95). A writer that finds checks holding the gate or the case waits for them to finish, up to a set time. One that finds another writer holding the case waits a moment and is then refused, and never queues behind another writer (revision 7). One whose expected log position is stale gets a conflict and reloads (R17). The lock belongs to a thread of a process, not to an object, and is recognised by the lock file itself, so two paths to one case folder, a symlink among them, are one case. The operating system drops a lock when its holder dies, so a crash never leaves a case locked. Since revision 13 (D124; R42), one save runs at a time per holder. The writer lock nests on its thread, so that a sign-off holds the case across its check and its append, and a save begun inside another on the same thread, from a signal handler, a finaliser or a callback, is refused before it writes anything. A save also refuses when the log grew since its check (R42), and a repair when the log or the head record changed at all (the lock files item below).
  - *What a reader reports* (revision 6, D74; revision 7 after verify-42; revision 8, D80; revision 9, D85, D87; revision 10, D95, D98; revision 11, D105; revision 12, D109; revision 16, D141; R43, P15). Whoever holds the case, sign-off and every append included, reads once and judges the whole log: a changed, removed or reordered entry is broken and an incomplete last entry is incomplete, each named. That read counts only while the case lock the holder holds and the gate it passed are still the case's; across a removed or replaced one, the holder reads again as any other reader below (R43 since revision 17, after verify-72; 0d339cc). Any other reader holds the gate shared for each attempt, so that no writer can take the case while it reads, and then tries the case lock shared. A check holds both shared, on Windows through LockFileEx, so checks never exclude each other.
    - If the case lock is free, or there is no case lock file behind the gate, no writer holds the case and none can take it: the reader reads once and judges the whole log. This is the only way a reader that does not already hold the case reports intact (D75).
    - If another writer holds the case lock, that writer held it when the reader looked, and the gate keeps every other writer out, so the same writer holds it until it lets go. The reader judges a read across which the head record did not change, then tries the case lock again. If the writer let go, the reader reads once holding the lock and judges the whole log, naming an incomplete entry. If not, something wrong at or before the recorded entries is broken, named; otherwise the reader reports the recorded entries it judged and the rest as being saved, never intact and never broken. With no steady read, it reports being saved and claims nothing about any entry. This is the only way a reader reports the recorded entries and the rest as being saved: one writer held the case through its read, because the reader held the gate (R43 since revision 12).
    - If the gate is busy, because a writer is passing it or paused while holding it, that writer has not taken the case (D98). The reader tries the case lock shared directly: if it gets it, the writer at the gate cannot take the case, so the reader reads once and judges the whole log, which counts only if the case lock it held is still the case's after the read (revision 16; R43 says so since revision 17, after verify-72, and 0d339cc compares it). If that case lock is missing when the reader compares it, it was removed during the read, and the reader looks again through the gate and the case lock, never reading as a reader that finds no gate file (R43 since revision 18, after verify-74; 7c8fba2). If the case lock is held too, the reader does not hold the gate, so it cannot know that one writer holds the case through its read: the holder can let go, leaving an incomplete entry, and the writer at the gate can take the case between the reader's looks. From a steady read it names a change, removal or reordering at or before the recorded entries, which is wrong whoever holds the case (D105). Otherwise it claims nothing from that read and tries again (D109; R43 and P15 since revision 12).
    - If there is no gate file, no writer has ever started on the case, because a writer creates the gate before the case lock, and a check creates nothing. A steady read stands unless a gate appeared during it; then the reader tries again.
    - When its retries run out, every attempt having found the gate busy and the case held, or a gate appearing, the reader reports the case as being saved and claims nothing about any entry. An attempt that finds the case free always ends with a verdict on the whole log, so a reader never ends without one on a case it found free.
    - A lock the reader's own thread holds through another handle or path is not another holder, so such a reader reads once and judges the whole log.
    - A lock that cannot be taken or tested is not another holder either (D50). The reader believes a steady read as it stands, reporting an incomplete entry as incomplete. With no steady read it reports that the log could not be checked because the lock could not be tested, claims nothing about any entry and names no other window.
    - A read counts only while the lock files the reader holds through it, the gate, the case lock or both, are still the case's (revision 16, D141, after verify-70; R43, P15). After each read the reader compares each file it holds with what the path names now: each in turn and then the first again, so that neither is judged only from a look taken before the other's. A reader that holds the case also checks that each file it holds still has a name (R43 and P15 since revision 22, D192, after verify-102; 523c5df). If one was removed or replaced, the reader takes the read again, through the gate and the case lock as at its first attempt (R43 since revision 18), and claims nothing from that one, and if every attempt goes so, it claims nothing about any entry. A lock file replaced before the read is not one the reader holds: a writer that passed the old gate and paused before the case lock can take the case through it, and a reader holding the new gate cannot see that. R43 and P15 say since revision 17 that, beyond a read across a lock file the reader counts on, such interference is outside what a reader can know, and that a check made after it stops gives the true answer (D144). The check is itself a sequence of looks, so a lock file removed or replaced, renamed aside and replaced included, after the check's last look at it is outside what any check can know, as the window between a writer's check and its step is (R43 and P15 since revision 22).
    No writer state is read to decide any of this: the lock files hold no data.
  - *Damage the head record shows* (revision 9, D85, D87): the first append writes an empty head record before its line, and since revision 15 creates it only where none exists, linking it into place so that it is never renamed over one (after verify-68), so every entry on disk has a head record beside it, and a missing head record beside any entry is damage, named as the first bad entry, never a torn first append to drop. A recorded entry whose line is cut short is damage to a recorded entry. Every reader names it as the first bad entry, with the reason that the head record lists it and its line is cut short: R43's incomplete last entry when the holder or a reader of a free case finds it last, and a change at or before the recorded entries for a reader while another writer holds the case. `cma case check` exits 3 for it either way. It is never offered for dropping, because the head record lists it. Only an entry past the recorded entries can be a crash's torn write, and only a holder may confirm dropping it.
  - *The lock files are the tool's own* (revision 10, D98, D99; revision 11, D108; revision 13, D124; revision 14, D125; revision 15, D136; revision 16, D141; R42, P15). The gate and the case lock sit beside the log and hold no data. Before each step that changes a file a reader reads, a writer re-checks that both are still the files it took: the gate it passed, whose identity it noted then, and the case lock it holds (D108; R42 and P15 since revision 12; the gate in R42 since revision 15, after verify-68). Since 523c5df the re-check is the holder's check: both paths, the link counts of both files it holds, and both paths again, the case lock last, immediately before the step. A reader judges by the gate whether a writer has started and whether one held the case through its read, so a writer that went on after the gate was removed or replaced would write into a case the reader judges by a file the writer never passed. Those steps are the empty head record that precedes the first line, the line, and each rename that publishes a head record or a repaired log. Writing a temporary file that nobody reads is not such a step. If either was removed or replaced, the writer refuses the step. Refused before the line, it writes nothing of its entry and says the save was not recorded, as R42 asks of every save not told recorded; at 523c5df, as at 7fb3a23, that refusal says only that the step was refused (Context, T12's reviews after revision 21). Refused once any part of the line is written, a short write's second call included, it leaves that part past the recorded entries as a torn entry, as a crash leaves it, says the save was not recorded, never that nothing was written, in no part of its report (R42 since revision 13; partway through the line in R42 and P15 since revision 19, D158, after verify-76; 9c265a3; every part of the report since revision 20, D163, after verify-78; de6a56e), and the next holder confirms dropping it. A writer also refuses a lock file replaced between opening and locking it. No file system lets a check and the step after it happen as one step, so a window remains. Someone who removes or replaces a lock file while a writer is between its check and its step can let a second writer take the case and save too. That is outside R42's single-writer guarantee, as hand-editing the log is (D99), and R42 says what holds instead (D124). Two more checks make it hold (D125; R42 since revision 14; built in 1d16873):
    - *A save is told recorded only after it reads back*, once its head record is published, an intact log that holds its entry where that head record lists it. Otherwise it says the save was not recorded and that the case should be checked. A save also publishes its head record only if the head record still lists what its check read. If the read-back fails, the save takes back the head record it published, when that head record is still its own, by putting back the head record its check read (revision 16, D141, after verify-70; R42); the take-back is a head rename, re-checked as every head rename is (D149; R42 since revision 17; 7c8fba2). Its entry is then past the recorded entries, as a crash leaves it, and the next holder confirms dropping it. A head record another writer has published since is left alone. Suppose the take-back is not made, because the head record is no longer the save's own, a lock file was removed or replaced, or the head record cannot be read or renamed. The entry then stays where it is, and the save is reported as not confirmed, neither recorded nor not recorded, with a request to check the case log, which shows whether the entry is among the recorded entries (D143; R42 and P15 since revision 17). That report names what stopped the take-back in the save's own words: the head record no longer the save's own, a lock file removed or replaced, or the operating-system error, named. It never quotes the take-back's refusal, and no part of it, its causes included, says the save was recorded, was not recorded or that nothing was written (D164; R42 since revision 21, D168; 7fb3a23, which neither quotes nor chains the refusal, src/cma/case.py:1106-1121).
    - *A repair re-checks*, immediately before it renames the repaired log over the log, that the log, byte for byte and not only by its length (after verify-68), and the head record are still as its check read them, and immediately before it renames its head record, that the head record still is; it refuses otherwise. The log is then the one its own rename published, which its read-back checks, as every save's does.
    Each check leaves a window before its step, as every check does, but the two cover each other. For a save to be told recorded and then lose its entry to a repair, the repair's log rename must follow the save's read-back, and then the repair's re-check before its head rename finds the save's head record and refuses. What the tool keeps as far as it can (revision 13 made these guarantees; since revision 23 they are what it tries under such interference, not what it promises, D210):
    - Two saves told recorded at one position leave two entries that claim it, or a head record that lists one entry beside a log that holds another, so the head record contradicts one of them or the second's link does not name the entry before it. The holder and every reader of a free case report the log broken, naming the entry, and sign-off and appends refuse it until it is resolved. A reader while another writer holds the case reports being saved for what follows the recorded entries. No reader reports it intact.
    - Under such interference a log that reads intact holds every save told recorded. A repair may remove the entry of a save told it was not recorded, as it removes a crash's torn write, and the log then reads intact without it (D124; verify-64's schedule); the person saves again.
    - Under such interference a reader takes a read again when a lock file it holds was removed or replaced during it (revision 16), but a lock file replaced before its read can still let it mistake a live append for damage, or report being saved over an entry a crash left; a check after the interference stops gives the true answer, as R43 and P15 say since revision 17 (D144).
    - A writer that takes the case after a take-back's re-check checked a head record that lists the save's entry, so its own entry follows that entry. A take-back that lands over its head record leaves two or more entries past the recorded ones, and the log reads broken, never intact (revision 17).
    - A hand edit of the log and its head record together can still remove a recorded save; a signed pack pins the head it saw (D103).
  - *A save the file system fails* (revision 18, D153, after verify-74; R42). After its check, a save reads, writes, syncs, links and renames files, and the file system can fail any of these: a full disk, an I/O error, a file system without hard links. The save then answers, naming the error, and the error itself never reaches its caller. Before the rename that publishes its head record, the save is not recorded: whatever part of its line was written stays past the recorded entries, as a crash leaves it, for the next holder to drop. After that rename the head record lists the save, and if the folder that holds it cannot then be synced, a power cut could still undo the rename, so the save is not confirmed and asks for the case log to be checked before anything else is saved; it makes no read-back or take-back then, since a take-back would need the same sync to last. A read-back whose read fails is a failed read-back, taken back as above, and a failure in a take-back answers not confirmed. Taking the case comes before the save's check, so a lock that cannot be taken is as R43 says: the append fails with the lock's error and writes nothing. Since revision 19 R42 states this as one rule (D158): every operating-system error a save meets after its check, in a step or in a read it makes to re-check or read itself back, answers not recorded, naming the error, unless the save's head record was published and not taken back, when it answers not confirmed. 7c8fba2 builds it where the head record is published, telling the two answers apart by whether the head record still lists what the save's check read (_publish, src/cma/case.py:1056-1066 at 51c8b68), and 51c8b68 at every other step that changes a file (_os_errors_as_not_recorded, :842-852). Since revision 20 R42 names the reads a save makes only to check: the comparison of the log with its check, the re-checks of the head record, the read-back and the examination of both lock files. One that cannot be made is reported as its error, never as a conflict to reload or a lock file removed or replaced, and a lock file missing when the writer examines it was removed (D159, D163). 1e35352 builds it (Context, T12's twenty-seventh review). Since revision 21 R42 says that whether the head record's rename was made is known from the rename, never inferred from reading the head record back (D168, after verify-80): a head record that cannot be read in the re-check before its rename stops the rename, and the save answers not recorded. 26bc51e builds it: the save notes when its rename is done and answers from that (src/cma/case.py:1009, :1076 at 7fb3a23). At 1e35352 such a save answered not confirmed.
  - *Scope of the guarantees* (revision 23, D210; R42, R43, P15). Removing, renaming or replacing a case lock file while the case is in use, and interrupting a save at an arbitrary instant (a signal, Ctrl-C, or a handler, callback or finaliser that runs inside the save, a nested save from one included), are outside the single-writer and reader guarantees. Under them the re-checks, the read-back, the take-back and the reader's checks and rereads above are how the tool tries, and it reports broken, being saved, not recorded or not confirmed as it can. A check of the case log made after the interference stops gives the true answer about the log as it then stands, and a signed pack pins the head it saw. The guarantees cover every case with no such interference: one writer at a time, one save at a time per holder, a save told recorded only after its read-back, the not-recorded and not-confirmed answers when the file system fails, and a holder, a sign-off or a reader that finds the case free naming any bad or incomplete entry. A save begun inside another on the same thread is still refused before it writes anything (a guarantee, which D212 keeps). Nothing is removed from the build: 43332c6 keeps every check it has.
  - *Not supported, stated rather than detected*: forking a process while it holds a case's lock (the tool never forks; a forked child's writes through the inherited CaseLog are refused, and the child leaving a writer block releases nothing of its parent's); case folders on a network share, where the operating system may emulate the lock per process and so not separate two CaseLog objects in one process (D66); removing, renaming or replacing the lock files while the case is in use (revision 10, D99; renaming named since revision 23, D210); and interrupting a save at an arbitrary instant (revision 23, D210).
- **C4 Importers** (src/cma/importers/). A shared step that walks and verifies the balance, extracted from monzo.py; new OBIE v3.1, TrueLayer and bank-CSV importers. Every importer writes the five-column file that ingest.load_csv validates. The loader and every importer read amounts and balances as exact decimals, count them in whole pence, and refuse a statement with an amount or balance, or a money in or money out, of £10,000,000,000,000 or more, naming the line or the total (R2 since revision 25). The loader does so since 0c63ca4 and the Monzo importer since aa04f0c, each naming which flow reached the limit since 3886917. Since revision 29 (D258) every refusal names a line by the line of the file its record starts on, blank lines counted, and a field too long to read only as too long to read (read_records, shared by the loader and the Monzo importer). The Monzo importer checks its options and the export's header, then reads rows in order, a row's currency and then, for a row that counts, its amount before its other fields, and stops at the first problem, saying with it whether money in or money out is at the limit among the rows read so far. An import into a case of more than one account is also refused when it would bring the money in of the case's accounts together, or their money out together, each account counted in full before own-account pairs are removed, to the limit, naming the account and which total would reach it, and the case is left unchanged (R41 since revision 26, D224; Tier 2). Since revision 30 (D289) the loader and the Monzo importer read a file into memory, decode it and split it into lines before its records, then read the records one at a time, in one pass, one record ahead (ingest.iter_data); the Monzo importer checks no record after the one it stops on. Since revision 31 (D301) that limit is about each record's own values. The checks that relate records to one another (the file's direction and date order, the balance chain, the totals and the Monzo importer's walk of the balance after sorting) are made from the records read once reading has stopped.
- **C5 Review workspace** (new src/cma/review/). A loopback server and one bundled page with no build step. The server is one process that serves requests on threads and never forks workers, so every window's writes stay inside the case log's scope (R42). It renders what C2 and policy.evaluate computed and never computes money. The override form offers only categories valid for the line's direction. While show_policy_verdict is on, a policy-check panel shows the outcome and each rule's result on the current figures, with the illustrative label in the panel. The judgement queue comes first (R15). For an ambiguous credit it shows each candidate and the bucket it would give: a table rule by its list, a payment-method candidate as giving no bucket, and a profile candidate as a name from the case profile with the bucket its kind gives, so the underwriter's override starts from the candidates. The drill-down shows each line's rule as recorded, which for an ambiguous credit is the conflict form (R16). While another window is saving, the case view says the case is being saved; it shows a broken-log warning only for something wrong at or before the recorded entries, or for an incomplete entry once it can judge the whole log (R43). A save refused because a case lock file, the gate or the case lock, was removed or replaced says so, says that the save was not recorded, and asks the underwriter to reload and save again. So does a save refused because the log changed since its check or while it was being recorded, or because another save is under way in the same window (R42). A save whose read-back failed says the save was not recorded; one whose head record was not then taken back says that whether the save is in the log is not confirmed, names what stopped the take-back, and asks the underwriter to check the case before saving again (R42 since revision 17, D143); no part of that message, nor any error it carries, says the save was recorded, was not recorded or that nothing was written (R42 since revision 21, D164, D168). A save that met an operating-system error names it and says that the save was not recorded or, when its head record was published and not taken back, that whether it is in the log is not confirmed (R42 since revision 18, D153; one rule since revision 19, D158). A save refused partway through its line says that part of its entry was written and that the save was not recorded (R42 since revision 19). Once any part of a save's line is in the log, no message about the save says nothing was written. A save whose own check could not read the log, the head record or a lock file names that error, and never asks the underwriter to reload or says a lock file was removed or replaced (R42 since revision 20).
- **C6 Preview, pack and sign-off** (new src/cma/pack.py). Memo field map, deterministic section text, which quotes money to the whole pound as the memo's prose does while the record, the evidence and the monthly CSV carry it to the penny (R2 since revision 26; D230 kept the whole pound for the sections), the labelled policy-check section, grounding guard, HTML record, evidence JSON v1, monthly CSV, sign-off binding to the preview's snapshot, and an atomic write of the pack once, at sign-off. Sign-off checks the case log and appends its entry inside one hold of the case, so it judges the whole log and a broken or incomplete log refuses it, however the log looked to a reader a moment before (R43).
- **C7 Firm configuration** (new src/cma/firm.py). offline_lock (default on), show_policy_verdict (default on, D28), and the paths to firm rules, thresholds and field map. There is no timing setting (D32). Since revision 30 (6b43212, not yet reviewed), a reading of firm.yaml decides only for its own run and firm folder: building one hosted client, and the run's hosted requests and tracing while the run is going; a reading taken earlier, or under another firm root, allows nothing. Every other check reads firm.yaml as it happens: each MCP start, ASGI app start-up and request, and tool call. D291 chose that a lock turned on during a run takes effect from the next run, and that a resume reads firm.yaml as it resumes. R29 and P18 do not say so yet, and revision 31 asks when they should (Options considered).
- **C8 Verify** (new src/cma/verify.py). Recomputes an evidence record from its case and refuses records from another ruleset.
- **C9 Pseudonymiser** (new src/cma/pseudonymise.py).
- **C10 Synthetic data and evals** (src/cma/synth.py, evals/run_evals.py). New opt-in features, cash included, with truth for every bucket, line and indicator.
- **C11 Report** (CLI over C3). A local per-case summary with no personal data and no durations.
- **C12 Existing paths** (graph.py, cli.py, mcp_server.py, llm.py, policy.py). Behaviour unchanged but for `cma resume`, below; new fields are additive; the offline lock is checked. policy.evaluate and PolicyResult are reused as they are. `cma resume` decides a review that an earlier release left pending on the figures this release computes, and re-runs the grounding check. A policy rule whose figure differs takes its outcome from the stored policy result where that settles it, and otherwise from the policy file with the stored hash. If neither settles it, the review is refused, left pending, with nothing recorded (R53 since revision 28, D249; policy.reevaluate and graph.refreshed). Since revision 29 (D258), `cma` processes that share a checkpoint file take turns through a lock file beside it (NAME.lock) to start a review, to refresh a resumed one while it is still the pending one read, and to apply a decision, and a decided review's memo and evidence are made in full before either is written and moved into place, evidence first (cli._review_lock, and the export in cli._review). Since revision 30 (D289), while the lock is off, the MCP server's sse_app() and streamable_http_app() hand out the SDK's app behind a lock check that reads the lock at start-up and before each request and, once the lock is on, fails start-up, answers 503 and closes websockets; under the lock both refuse (mcp_server.LockCheckedASGI).

| R4 | C1 (stages, payment-method cues, whole words, separators, every occurrence, the longest match at each start, the bank's code, verdict, category order, candidates with the profile's, precedence, rule tables and their validation, whose exception checks only the pinned frozen v1 table, as its bytes were verified, is spared, judged by its bytes first, which names a malformed value by rule, and which builds every rule's matchers, naming a pattern that cannot be used with the other faults, and which names a key given more than once and checks every value it was given, sparing v1 only by a digest that matches the pin; and, since revision 23, a table that is not well formed refused as a whole, naming at least one fault) |

Every active requirement maps to a component; R49 is withdrawn (D32). Two things in the bet are not system requirements and have no component: the outcome metric itself, hours per application, which the owner and the friend read by stopwatch (D32), and the approver's answer (D25, E1, E5).

### Out of scope

- PDF extraction. An estimated majority of files are PDF, but there is no sample layout, and extraction is where invented numbers come from. When a layout arrives, it plugs into the importer contract and the balance-chain check.
- Plaid and Yapily importers, until a sample of the firm's feed exists (E7, D30).
- Any credit decision or recommendation. The illustrative policy check is shown, labelled (D28), and never fills the recommendation or any other judgement field.
- Loading the firm's own lending policy into the workspace or the pack, until the firm supplies one; `cma run --policy` keeps that option.
- Timing the underwriter in the tool, or deriving durations from the case log (D32).
- A firm setting that takes cash deposits out of trading receipts (see the cash sub-decision).
- A score, a model or a rule order that picks between the candidates for an ambiguous credit (Option E and the D52 sub-decision).
- Firm rules that settle an ambiguous credit.
- Profile names that settle an ambiguous credit, and telling the payer from a name in the payment reference (revision 7, D78): a bank line has no separate payer field.
- An order, or a longest-name rule, between the two kinds of profile name one credit matches (revision 9, D86); and checking a profile for one name entered under both kinds.
- Approximate matching of listed names (revision 6): a name matches as listed, apart from the separators C1 names.
- A judgement queue in `cma run`'s terminal review (revision 4). `cma run` is not a case: under v2 it records each ambiguous credit's conflict and candidates in its evidence lines and counts the credit in M16, and the queue belongs to the workspace (R15). `cma run` has no profile, so its candidates never include a profile name, and no credit is ambiguous there because of one.
- Forking a process while it holds a case's lock, and a workspace server or worker pool that forks (revision 5). The tool never forks; a forked child's writes through an inherited CaseLog are refused.
- Case folders on a network share or a mapped drive, and detecting one (revision 5, D66). R32 warns only for known sync clients; the README and the trial sheet say local disk.
- Case folders on a file system without hard links, such as FAT or exFAT (revision 15). The first append links its empty head record into place, which fails there; the save writes nothing and, since revision 18, answers not recorded, naming the file system's error (R42); the README and the trial sheet name the file systems to use.
- Making a head record durable when the folder that holds it cannot be synced (revision 18). The save answers not confirmed, and a check of the case log gives the log's answer at that moment.
- Telling a live append from a torn entry left by a crash while another writer holds the case (revision 6, D74). A reader in that moment reports the case as being saved; the holder, or a reader once the case is free, decides.
- Removing or replacing a case's lock files while the case is in use, and detecting the cleanup, sync or backup tool that does it (revision 10, D99). A writer re-checks its lock before each step a reader reads; two saves told recorded at one position leave a broken log. A repair under such interference may remove only the entry of a save told it was not recorded (D124). A reader takes a read again only when a lock file it counts on was removed or replaced during it (revision 16; a holder's and a busy-gate read since revision 17); beyond that, interference is outside what a reader can know, and a check after it stops gives the true answer (D144).
- Naming every fault, or every combination of a repeated key's values, in a rule table that is not well formed (revision 23, D210). Such a table is refused as a whole, naming at least one fault, and nothing loads from it.
- Any guarantee about what a save or a reader reports, or what the log holds, while a case lock file is removed, renamed or replaced, or after a save is interrupted at an arbitrary instant (revision 23, D210). The tool reports as it can; a check after the interference stops gives the true answer, and a signed pack pins the head it saw.
- A log file copied or hard-linked outside its case folder. It is another log with its own lock, not another path to the case.
- Companies House, the credit bureau, or any other network lookup.
- Hosting, sign-in, multi-tenancy, and a data processing agreement.
- Merchant cash advance repayments taken out of card settlements before payout; they never appear as a line.
- A seasonality-aware trend (24 months, year on year).
- DOCX output. Sections are copied as plain or rich text until the firm's template arrives (E8).
- A money-out breakdown beyond the existing figures and the monthly totals.
- Statements with an amount or balance, or a money in or money out, of £10,000,000,000,000 or more (revision 25, D223). They are refused at import and no figure is reported from them; carrying money in the models as integer pence or decimals, which would lift the limit, is not done (Options considered).
- Money quoted to the penny in the memo's prose, the drafting prompt's metrics table or the policy check's rule sentences (revision 26, D229); the evidence and every table carry the pence. The pack's memo-ready sections quote whole pounds too (D230).
- Reproducing the previous release's binary-float arithmetic where it came to other than the exact pence (revision 27, D234). On such a statement M1 to M16 and their evidence follow the exact pence, so a v1 rollback there does not reproduce a penny the previous release's floats made; the previous release's lines are kept wherever its sums were exact.
- Resuming a review an earlier release left pending whose policy outcome neither its stored result nor the policy file it was drafted with settles, and storing a copy of every policy in the checkpoint (revision 28, D249). `cma resume` refuses such a review, naming the figure, and the reviewer runs `cma run` on the statement again.
- Naming every problem in a Monzo export, and saying whether rows after the first problem would bring money in or money out to the limit (revision 29, D258). The importer stops at its first problem.
- Another program writing to a checkpoint file, removing, renaming or replacing its lock file, and a `cma` process stopped part-way through a write (revision 29, D258).
- A disk failure, a full disk, an interruption or an output path taken meanwhile between a recorded decision and both files in place, and a second export of a decided review (revision 29, D258). The decision stays recorded in the checkpoint.
- Reading a statement or an export as a stream, so that nothing after the record a refusal names is read, and checking a record before the next one has been read (revision 30, D289). A file is read into memory, decoded and split into lines before its first record is checked, as the previous release read every record before checking any; a file too large to read into memory is outside this release.
- Making the checks that relate a statement's records to one another within one record of the records they relate, and any order between such a check and a record's own problem beyond what R2 says the Monzo importer names with its first problem (revision 31, D301). Those checks are which way the file runs and whether its dates keep to it, whether each balance follows from the one before, the money in and money out totals, and the Monzo importer's walk of the balance after sorting. They are made from the records read once reading has stopped.
- A listening socket that an embedder's own ASGI server opens or holds for an MCP app it saved while the lock was off, before the app has started up, as with lifespan events turned off or a socket handed to it already bound (revision 30, D289). Under the lock the app serves no request and no tool runs; the socket is the embedder's to close.
- Any claim about the market. This is for one firm and one underwriter.

### Cut line for 2026-11-29 (decided, D33)

Tier 1, demonstrating the mechanism (D23): R1-R7, R10-R19, R22-R31, R33-R35, R42, R43, R47, R48, R52-R56 (39 requirements), plus the stopwatch trial sheet (documentation only). It covers the v2 tables with real acquirers, lenders and cash wording and the two-stage verdict with the ambiguity policy, the buckets with cash inside trading receipts, the monthly summary and indicators, single-account cases with a profile and an override log, the workspace with the judgement queue and the illustrative policy check, the preview, pack and sign-off, the case log within its stated scope, the offline lock, and a synthetic case shaped like a business.
Tier 2: R8, R9, R20, R21, R32, R36-R41, R44-R46, R50, R51 (16 requirements). It covers the importers, multiple accounts, firm rules, verify, the pseudonymiser, the report and the comparison.

## Consequences

**Gains**
- The underwriter gets the view they asked for: £80k in, of which £55k is trading and the other £25k is explained by bucket. Every figure is clickable, and their own corrections are on record.
- A cash business is not understated: takings paid in as cash count as trading, the cash inside them is its own figure, and the deposits a person should look at come first (D31).
- No silent choice between lists: when the shipped lists disagree about a credit, the underwriter sees the line, every rule it matched in either stage and the bucket each would give, so a name the cues outranked is still in view (D60). Neither a credit's bucket nor its category depends on the order of rules within a stage, which a test checks by shuffling them and recategorising whole statements (D52, revision 4); a debit takes the first matching rule in table order, as it always has. Since revision 10 the order of alternatives inside a pattern picks nothing either (D102, and R4 since revision 11).
- No silent choice by a name in a reference (revision 7, D78): a director or an own account named anywhere in an ambiguous credit's description shows as one more reading in the queue instead of settling it, so a customer's payment that mentions the director is seen by a person.
- No silent choice between the profile's two kinds of name (revision 9, D86): a credit that names both a director and an own account shows both readings, and every rule it matched, in the queue instead of being filed as director funding.
- R5 and R6 say the precedence the categoriser follows (revisions 8 and 9), so T05 is judged on text that matches T03's build: a refund, a loan or a returned payment that names a director or an own account keeps what its wording says, and a credit both kinds claim is ambiguous. Since revision 11 R4 likewise says how the bank's code and a pattern's alternatives meet the longer-phrase rule, so T03 is judged on text that matches D100's build. Since revision 12 R42, R43 and P15 do the same for T12: they name the steps a writer re-checks its lock before (D108), and say that a reader that cannot know one writer held the case through its read names only a fault at or before the recorded entries (D109). Since revision 13 they also say what a save is told under lock-file interference, and run one save at a time per holder (D124), so T12 is judged on text that names the repair boundary. Since revision 14 R42 says when a save is told recorded and what a repair re-checks (D125), and R4 that cash wording is never hidden inside a payment-method phrase and that no v2 rule carries an exception (D126, D127), so both tasks are judged on text that says what 1d16873, 1b592b6 and 4adddf9 build. Since revision 15 R4 says that cash paid-in wording and a payment-method cue never take each other in and that a lookaround is an exception (D132, D136), and R42 that the re-check covers the gate (D136), so both tasks are judged on text that says what 78556a7 and cd298cc build. Since revision 16 R4 says that an anchor is refused except on the bank's code and that only a match that itself counts sets another aside, and R42, R43 and P15 that a failed read-back takes its head record back and that a read across a changed lock file is taken again (D137, D141), so both tasks are judged on text that says what 5c9a5bd, 2c72469 and f5436bb build. Since revision 17 R4 says that the set-aside rule is judged match by match (D142). R42 and P15 say that a save whose take-back is not made is not confirmed (D143). R43 and P15 say that a holder's and a busy-gate read count only while their lock files are the case's, and that interference before a read is outside what a reader can know (D144). So both tasks are judged on text that says what 4b3b21c, 0d339cc and 3076547 build, apart from the take-back's lock re-check, which revision 17 keeps and D149 confirms. Since revision 18 R42 says how a save answers when the file system fails one of its steps, R43 that a read taken again goes through the locks, and R4 that only the pinned v1 table is spared the table checks (D153), so both tasks are judged on text that says what 7c8fba2 and 9042fb4 build, apart from the file system's errors at a save's other steps, which R42 now reaches and revision 18 asks about. Since revision 19 R42 and P15 say that a save refused partway through its line is not recorded, never nothing written, and R42 states one rule for every operating-system error a save meets after its check; R4 and R54 say that a changed v1 is refused as changed before anything else is checked, and R4 that a malformed value is named by rule (D158). So both tasks are judged on text that says what 9c265a3, 51c8b68 and 7cb2e71 build, apart from the reads a save makes only to check, which revision 19 asks about, and the one validator path in 7cb2e71 that still raises a type error. Since revision 20 R42 names the reads a save makes only to check. R42 and P15 say that no part of a save's report says nothing was written once its line is in the log. R4 says that a malformed value on a bank-code rule is named by its rule, and that any failure of a changed v1 is named as changed first (D163). So both tasks are judged on text that says what de6a56e, 1e35352 and 5139e22 build, apart from the words a not-confirmed report quotes, which revision 20 asks about. Since revision 21 R42 says that each report gives one answer, its causes included, and that whether a head record was published is known from its rename, and R4 that a changed v1 names its exceptions and that every rule's matchers are built while the table is checked (D168). So both tasks are judged on text that says what 26bc51e, 7fb3a23 and 5f5bb2e build, apart from the refusal before any part of a save's line, which 26bc51e words as a refused step, and an unusable pattern beside another malformed field, which 5f5bb2e does not yet name. Since revision 22 R43 and P15 say how a reader's check looks at the lock files and what no check can see. R4 says that only a matching digest spares v1, and that a key given more than once is named with every value checked (D192). So both tasks are judged on text that says what 523c5df and 4c017d4 build, apart from the refusal before any part of a save's line, which 523c5df still words as a refused step, and the values of a repeated field that 4c017d4 does not yet check. Since revision 23 both tasks are judged on text whose scope leaves out the corners of their latest majors: verify-118 needs a lock file replaced and a save interrupted, and verify-119 a table that is not well formed (D210). The refusal before any part of a save's line comes only from a lock file removed or replaced, now outside R42's guarantee, and the values of a repeated field are now outside R4.
- Each figure says how its lines prove it (revision 24, D219): a sum adds up to its lines, an outflow to its lines negated, and a derived figure shows its inputs and method, M9 naming the lines it counts only in part. The underwriter checking a figure by hand, and a reviewer judging P2, know which sum to do, and M1-M16 keep the values and signs that signed memos and the packaged policy read. Since revision 25 M1-M16 also keep the evidence the previous release gave them, and every money figure is the counted pence: a statement whose amounts or totals no float carries to the penny is refused where it enters, never reported a penny out (D223). Since revision 26 the requirements say where the pence show, in the evidence and every table, while the memo's prose keeps the whole pound the previous release quoted; and what the previous release ran, one statement under v1 with no case profile, which M1 to M16 match (D229). Since revision 27 they match its lines only where its float sums came to the exact pence; where they did not, each figure and its evidence follow the pence the lines hold, never a penny the floats made (D234). Since revision 28 a review an earlier release left pending is decided on those figures too. Its policy outcome comes from the policy it was drafted with, or `cma resume` refuses it and says to draft again (D249). Since revision 29 a refusal names the line a person sees, and a Monzo refusal says money in or money out at the limit among the rows it read. `cma` processes sharing a checkpoint file take turns, so a review is decided once, and no memo is put in place without its evidence (D258). Since revision 30 the requirements say how far the reader reads before it names a first problem, the whole file and one record ahead, as far as the previous release read, and an MCP app saved before the lock was turned on serves nothing once it is (D289). Since revision 31 they also say which checks wait for the reading to stop: a record's own values are checked within one record, and the checks across records once reading has stopped, which is when the previous release made all of them (D301).
- The bank's typography no longer hides a list (revision 6): a hyphen, a typographic dash, a dot or an invisible character inside a listed name changes nothing, and a cheque with cash wording is ambiguous by the same verdict as the rail, not by an exception, and since revision 7 by R4's own text (D70-D72, D76).
- A table edit cannot give payer wording the bank's standing (D96, D97), a table that would fail on a credit fails when it loads instead (D94), and since D110 a pattern cannot reach across words to hide another list. Since D115 to D121 a table edit cannot switch the bank's code off, a pattern whose longest match cannot be found or that matches no text is refused, and a rule id cannot blur a line's provenance. Since D126 no rule can carry an exception that hides another list's wording, and since D136 and D137 no pattern can carry one as a lookahead, a lookbehind or an anchor. Since D153 a table cannot skip those checks by calling itself frozen: only v1's pinned bytes are frozen. Since D158 a changed v1 is refused as changed before anything else in it is checked, and no malformed value is to escape as a type error. Since D163 that holds whatever else a malformed rule carries and whatever a change to v1 makes fail. Since D168 a changed v1 names the exceptions it gained, and a pattern that cannot be used is named with the table's other faults. Since D192 nothing spares a table but a digest of its bytes that matches the pin, and a key given twice is named while every value it was given is still checked, so a repeat hides no fault. Since D210 (revision 23) R4 asks of a table that is not well formed only that it is refused whole, naming at least one fault, which held through every review since revision 19; naming by rule is for a well-formed table.
- Each task can now finish its own requirement: T03 delivers R4 through `cma run`'s evidence, T05 delivers R5 and R6 through the categoriser and the case pipeline, and the queue's tasks deliver R15 (D58, D60, revision 9).
- The case log has a stated boundary (D65, D99) and a reader that claims only what it saw held still (D74). The reader says intact only after finding the case free (revision 7). Since revision 10 it knows from the gate, not from a mark or a count, that the writer it found held the case through its read (D95), so the lock files hold no data. A writer refuses once it sees either lock file gone or replaced (the gate since revision 15), and two saves told recorded at one position under interference show as a broken log, so the review judges the lock against R42's text instead of searching for the next race between a check and a write. The fork machinery (D63), the lock-file marks (D69) and the hold counts (D93) are gone. Since revision 13 the boundary is stated by what a writer is told: a save told recorded is never missing from a log that reads intact, a save refused after its line says so, and a second save on one thread is refused (D124). Since revision 14 R42 also says how a writer knows: it reads back what it published before saying so (D125). Since revision 16 a save told not recorded leaves its entry where the next holder can drop it, and a reader re-reads when a lock file it holds changes under it (D141). Since revision 17 a save whose head record is not taken back says it is not confirmed instead of not recorded, so the tool never tells a person a save was not recorded while the log holds it (D143). Since revision 18 a save the file system fails answers too, naming the error, so a caller always hears recorded, not recorded or not confirmed (D153). Since revision 19 a save refused partway through its line says it was not recorded, never that nothing was written, so a person is never told a case is untouched when it needs a repair (D158). Since revision 20 no part of a save's report says that, whatever stopped the save. A save that cannot read what it checks names the error, never another window's save or a removed lock file (D159, D163). Since revision 21 each report gives one answer, its causes included, so a person told a save is not confirmed is never also told it was or was not recorded, and a save whose head record cannot be read before its rename is told it was not recorded, never not confirmed (D164, D168). Since revision 22 R43 and P15 state what a reader's check of its lock files can see. A review then judges the check against a stated window instead of moving the replacement past whichever look comes last (D192). Since revision 23 lock-file interference and a save interrupted at an arbitrary instant are outside the guarantees altogether (D210), so a review judges T12 on cases with no such interference. The last findings there, an unchecked read turned into a broken log and an entry named with no recorded count (verify-104, verify-108), are fixed at 4dc43f8 and 33cfac2.
- The showcase runs the whole chain, from traced figures to an illustrative pass, refer or fail that names the rules a person must look at, and the label says whose decision it is (D28).
- The approver's question, does data leave the firm, has an answer the code enforces: offline lock on, the MCP server refuses to start, and the evidence states offline mode. An MCP app an embedder saved while the lock was off serves no request once the lock is on, and its listening socket, if the embedder's server opened one, is the embedder's (revision 30, D289).
- Firm rules build up in a file the firm owns. That is the only switching cost any board lens found.
- The bet's outcome is read by stopwatch on comparable files (D32). For each case, the report adds the size of the judgement queue, the indicator counts and the overrides by kind. Those explain the minutes without the tool measuring the person.

**Costs and risks**
- The scope is large for two weeks and $20, and Tier 1 alone is most of it. T03 has used its attempts. Since revision 21 it has failed eleven more reviews: verify-103 failed 792727b on a repeated rules key, a repeated pattern that did not compile and a check given no digest. The owner's review-38 fix (4c017d4) waits for its review, and its unblock, D191, is pending (D64, D70-D72, D78, D86, D94, D96, D97, D100, D110, D115, D117, D119, D121, D123, D126, D132, D136, D137, D141, D168, D171, D173, D175, D177, D179, D181, D183, D185, D187, D189, D191, D192). T12 has used its attempts too. Since revision 21 it has failed eleven more reviews: verify-102 failed 57984ea on a gate moved aside and replaced.
